Applications of the L-space satellite formula
Daren Chen, Ian Zemke, Hugo Zhou

Abstract
. We give a formula for the τ \tau -invariant of a satellite knot P ⁡ ( K , n ) P(K,n) when P P is an L-space satellite operator. Our formula holds for general L-space satellite operators P P when the companion K K satisfies ε ⁡ ( K ) = 1 \varepsilon(K)=1 . When ε ⁡ ( K ) \varepsilon(K) is 0 0 or − 1 -1 , we state a formula which requires some additional assumptions on P P or n n . Our main tool is our algorithm which computes the knot Floer complex of satellite knots constructed using L-space satellite operators, which we developed in a previous paper [ 5 ] . Our formula for τ \tau recovers many existing formulas for the behavior of τ \tau under satellite operators, including for cables. We apply our formula to questions about the slice genus of satellite knots, showing, e.g., that if K K is a knot with τ ⁡ ( K ) = g 4 ​ ( K ) > 0 \tau(K)=g_{4}(K)>0 , then satellites of K K by L-space satellite operators have the same property. Another application is a proof that L-space satellite operators satisfy a conjecture of Hedden and Pinzón-Caicedo: If P P is an L-space satellite operator which acts as a group homomorphism on the smooth concordance group, then P P is either the zero operator, the identity operator, or the orientation reversing operator.



1.
Introduction

Satellite operations are one of the most basic constructions in knot theory. Suppose that
K\subseteq S^{3}
is a knot,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
is an integer, and
P\subseteq S^{1}\times D^{2}
is a knot in a solid torus. Then we can form a new knot,
P(K,n)\subseteq S^{3}
as follows. We identify
S^{1}\times D^{2}
with a tubular neighborhood of
K
in such a way that
S^{1}\times\{\theta\}
(for
\theta\in\partial D^{2}
) is sent to the
n
-framed longitude of
K
. Then
P(K,n)
is the image of
P
under the inclusion
S^{1}\times D^{2}\subseteq S^{3}
.

In this paper, we study satellite operators through the lens of Heegaard Floer theory.
Knot Floer homology
is an invariant of knots due independently to Ozsváth and Szabó
[
34
]
and Rasmussen
[
39
]
. One of the most basic concordance invariants that can be extracted from knot Floer homology is the
\tau
-invariant of Ozsváth and Szabó
[
33
]
. If
K\subseteq S^{3}
is a knot, then

|\tau(K)|\leq g_{4}(K),

where
g_{4}(K)
is the slice genus of
K
.

Many authors have studied how
\tau(K)
behaves under satellite operators. The behavior of
\tau
under cabling was determined by Hom
[
16
]
. For Whitehead doubles, this was understood by Hedden
[
14
]
. Many other authors have studied this question for specific satellite operators. See
[
21
,
12
,
1
,
38
]
for several examples.

In the present paper, we study the behavior of
\tau
under a family of satellite operators called
L-space satellite operators
. Our main technical result is a description of the behavior of
\tau
under L-space satellite operators when Hom’s
\varepsilon
-invariant
[
17
]
takes value 1 on the companion knot
K
. The cases when
\varepsilon(K)=0
or
-1
are more complicated, and we obtain partial results. This is stated in Theorem
1.2
.

Using the aforementioned result about
\tau
, we prove a number of topological applications. In Theorem
1.9
that we prove that L-space satellite operators only act by homomorphisms on the concordance group when the underlying operator is one of three basic operators: the zero operator, the identity operator, or the orientation reversing operator. We are also able to compute the slice genus of knots of the form
P(K,0)
when
P
is an L-space satellite operator and
K
is a knot with
\tau(K)=g_{4}(K)>0
.

1.1.
L-space satellite operators

If
P\subseteq S^{1}\times D^{2}
is a satellite operator, we write
L_{P}=\mu\cup P
for the 2-component link obtained by viewing the solid torus containing
P
as the neighborhood of an unknot in
S^{3}
. We assume that
S^{1}\times\{\theta\}
is sent to the 0-framed longitude of the unknot. The component
\mu
is a meridian of this solid torus.

If
P\subseteq S^{1}\times D^{2}
is a satellite operator, one defines the
winding number
of
P
to be the class of the knot
P
in
H_{1}(S^{1}\times D^{2})\cong\mathbb{Z}
. For our purposes, it is more convenient to identify this with the linking number
\ell=\mathrm{lk}(P,\mu)
of the link
L_{P}
.

In
[
5
]
, we studied the following family of satellite operators:

Definition 1.1
.

We say that a satellite operator
P
is an
L-space satellite operator
if the 2-component link
L_{P}
is an L-space link, i.e.,
S^{3}_{\Lambda}(L_{P})
is an L-space for all integral framings
\Lambda\gg(0,0)
.

In this paper, we study the full knot Floer complex of Ozsváth and Szabó
[
34
]
. This can be packaged in several ways, but we study its formulation as a free, finitely generated chain complex
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(K)
over the ring
\mathbb{F}[W,Z]
.

In
[
5
]
, we described a procedure for computing the knot Floer complex of
P(K,n)
in terms of the knot Floer complex of
K
when
P
is an L-space satellite operator. Our description is phrased in terms of the bordered theory from
[
44
,
45
]
, which is based on the link surgery formula
[
30
]
of Manolescu and Ozsváth. Therein, an associative algebra
\mathcal{K}
is described which encodes the Heegaard Floer surgery formulas. If
K\subseteq S^{3}
is a knot, a module
\mathcal{X}_{n}(K)^{\mathcal{K}}
over the algebra
\mathcal{K}
is described. To the negative Hopf link, there is a
DA
-bimodule
{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{H}_{-}^{\mathcal{K}}
over the algebra
\mathcal{K}
. Finally, if
L_{P}
is the 2-component link associated to a satellite operator
P
, then there is a
DA
-bimodule
{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L_{P})^{\mathbb{F}[W,Z]}
.
The gluing theorems from
[
44
]
imply that there is a homotopy equivalence

(1.1)

\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(P(K,n))^{\mathbb{F}[W,Z]}\simeq\mathcal{X}_{n}(K)^{\mathcal{K}}\boxtimes{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{H}_{-}^{\mathcal{K}}\boxtimes{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L_{P})^{\mathbb{F}[W,Z]}.

In
[
5
]
, we described a procedure for computing the bimodule
{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L_{P})^{\mathbb{F}[W,Z]}
when
P
is an L-space operator. In this case, the bimodule
{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L_{P})^{\mathbb{F}[W,Z]}
is computable from the multi-variable Alexander polynomials of
L_{P}
, and its sublinks. We implemented an algorithm to compute
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(P(K,n))^{\mathbb{F}[W,Z]}
based on Equation (
1.1
) into Python code, which is avaiable here
[
6
]
.

1.2.
Formulas for
\tau(P(K,n))

The main technical result of this paper is a formula for the behavior of
\tau
under L-space satellite operators, in many cases. Our result is phrased in terms of several familiar Heegaard Floer invariants. The first is Hom’s invariant
\varepsilon(K)\in\{-1,0,1\}

[
17
]
. Additionally, we consider the
H
-function of
L_{P}
, which we recall presently. If
L\subseteq S^{3}
is a 2-component link with components
L_{1},L_{2}
, we set

\mathbb{H}(L):=\left(\frac{\mathrm{lk}(L_{1},L_{2})}{2}+\mathbb{Z}\right)\times\left(\frac{\mathrm{lk}(L_{1},L_{2})}{2}+\mathbb{Z}\right).

The
H
-function of
L
is a map

H_{L}\colon\mathbb{H}(L)\to\mathbb{Z}^{\geq 0}.

It is defined as

H_{L}(\bm{\mathbf{s}})=-\frac{1}{2}\max\{\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(\bm{\mathbf{x}})|\bm{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathcal{H\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptL}(L,\bm{\mathbf{s}}),\mathbb{F}[U]\text{-non-torsion}\}.

In the above
\mathcal{H\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptL}(L)
denotes the homology of a version of link Floer homology over the four variable polynomial ring
\mathbb{F}[W_{1},Z_{1},W_{2},Z_{2}]
. The variable
U
acts by
U_{1}=W_{1}Z_{1}
or
U_{2}=W_{2}Z_{2}
. (Both variables
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
have the same action on homology).

It is helpful to define another numerical invariant
R_{t}\in\mathrm{lk}(L_{1},L_{2})/2+\mathbb{Z}
, ranging over
t\in\mathrm{lk}(L_{1},L_{2})/2+\mathbb{Z}
, via the formula

R_{t}=\max\left\{r\in\frac{\mathrm{lk}(L_{1},L_{2})}{2}+\mathbb{Z}\middle|\begin{array}[]{l}H_{L}(t,r+1)=H_{L}(t,r)\text{ and}\\
H_{L}(t,r-1)=H_{L}(t,r)+1\end{array}\right\}.

Our main technical result is the following:

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
P
be an L-space satellite operator, and let
L_{P}=\mu\cup P
be the associated 2-component L-space link. Orient the component
P
of
L_{P}
so that the linking number
\ell:=\mathrm{lk}(\mu,P)
is non-negative. Write
g_{3}(P)
for the Seifert genus of the pattern knot
P
(viewed as a knot in
S^{3}
). We have the following formula for
\tau(P(K,n))
:

(1)

Suppose
\varepsilon(K)=1
. Then

\tau(P(K,n))=\begin{cases}R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}-\frac{\ell}{2}+\frac{(\ell-1)\ell}{2}n+\ell\tau(K),&\text{if $n<2\tau(K),$}\\
g_{3}(P)+\frac{(\ell-1)\ell}{2}n+\ell\tau(K),&\text{if $n\geq 2\tau(K)$. }\end{cases}

(2)

Suppose
\varepsilon(K)=0.

(a)

If
n\geq 0
, then

\tau(P(K,n))=g_{3}(P)+\frac{(\ell-1)\ell}{2}n.

(b)

If
n<0
and the following condition holds for the
H
-function of
L_{P}
:

(1.2)

R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}\geq g_{3}(P)+\frac{\ell}{2}-1,

then

\tau(P(K,n))=\max\left\{R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}+\tfrac{\ell}{2},R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}-\tfrac{\ell}{2}\right\}+\tfrac{(\ell-1)\ell}{2}n

(3)

If
\varepsilon(K)=-1
, and Equation (
1.2
) holds,

then

\tau(P(K,n))=\begin{cases}\max\{R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}+\frac{\ell}{2},R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}-\frac{\ell}{2}\}+\frac{(\ell-1)\ell}{2}n+\ell\tau(K),&\text{if $n<2\tau(K),$}\\
\max\{R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}+\frac{\ell}{2},R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}-\frac{\ell}{2}\}+\frac{(\ell-1)\ell}{2}n+\ell\tau(K),&\text{if $n=2\tau(K)$. }\\
\min\{R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}+\frac{\ell}{2},R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}+\frac{\ell}{2}\}+\frac{(\ell-1)\ell}{2}n+\ell\tau(K),&\text{if $n=2\tau(K)+1$. }\\
\min\{R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}+\frac{\ell}{2},g_{3}(P)+\ell\}+\frac{(\ell-1)\ell}{2}n+\ell\tau(K),&\text{if $n>2\tau(K)+1$. }\end{cases}

Note that the condition that
\ell\geq 0
is very mild, since it can be obtained by reversing the orientation of
P\subseteq L_{P}
, and this operation preserves
\tau(P(K,n))
and does not affect
P
being an L-space satellite operator.

We describe a large number of examples in Section
8
, illustrating how Theorem
1.2
recovers many known formulas for the behavior of
\tau
under satellite operations. These include the following:

(1)

Hom’s formula for cables
[
16
]
;

(2)

Chen and Hanselman formula for satellites by 1-bridge braids
[
7
]
;

(3)

Hedden’s formula for the Whitehead pattern
[
14
]
;

(4)

Levine’s formula for the Mazur pattern
[
22
]
;

(5)

Bodish’s formula for a family of generalized Mazur patterns
[
1
]
(we recover only a subfamily of the patterns that Bodish considers);

(6)

Patwardhan and Xiao’s formula for a family of satellite patterns
P
, which have the property that the 2-component link
L_{P}
is a 2-bridge L-space link
[
38
]
.

The examples (3)–(6) fit into the family of patterns studied by Patwardhan and Xiao. These consist of certain patterns
P
where
L_{P}
is a two-bridge link of the form
L(rq-1,q)
, for
r,q
positive, odd integers. These are L-space links by
[
29
]
*Theorem 3.8, and furthermore we show that they satisfy the extra condition in Equation (
1.2
). In particular, the Whitehead pattern corresponds to the case
q=r=3
, and the Mazur pattern corresponds to
q=3,r=5
. Patwardhan and Xiao
[
38
]
study this family of operators with the extra assumption that the framing
n
is
0
.

The condition in Equation (
1.2
) seems somewhat restrictive. Nonetheless, we show that it is satisfied when
P
has linking number
\ell\in\{0,1\}
or if the pattern knot
P
(viewed as a knot in
S^{3}
) is unknotted. See Remark
5.17
. The condition is satisfied for the
(p,p+1)
cabling operator (therefore recovering Hom’s formula all
(p,pn+1)
-cables), but it is not satisfied for general cabling operators of the form
(p,pn+r)
for
r\neq\pm 1
. Therefore, the statement of Theorem
1.2
does not immediately recover Hom’s formula for the
\tau
invariant of the
(p,q)
-cable of a knot
K
with
\varepsilon(K)<0
. Nonetheless, we recall in Section
8
a simple argument using duality which recovers the formula when
\varepsilon(K)<0
from the formula when
\varepsilon(K)>0
. Similar arguments can be used in related settings to extend Theorem
1.2
in particular examples.

We will also give a topological interpretation of the quantity
R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}
in terms of the Thurston norm of the link complement of
L_{P}
:

Proposition 1.3
.

If
P
is an L-space satellite operator, then the quantity
R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}-|\ell|/2
is equal to the minimum genus of a surface in
S^{1}\times D^{2}
which has boundary equal to
P
and a disjoint union of
|\ell|
parallel copies of the longitude
S^{1}\times\{\theta\}
, for
\theta\in\partial D^{2}
. Throughout the paper, we denote this quantity by
g_{3}^{\rel}(P)
.

This is proven in Proposition
7.2
, below. We are unaware of a simple interpretation of
R_{t}
for
t\neq\frac{\ell}{2}
in terms of the Thurston norm of
L_{P}
.

Note that using Proposition
1.3
, the first part of Theorem
1.2
can be rephrased as claiming that if
\varepsilon(K)=1
and
P
is an L-space satellite operator with
\ell\geq 0
, then

\tau(P(K,n))=\begin{cases}g_{3}^{\rel}(P)+\frac{(\ell-1)\ell}{2}n+\ell\tau(K),&\text{if $n<2\tau(K),$}\\
g_{3}(P)+\frac{(\ell-1)\ell}{2}n+\ell\tau(K),&\text{if $n\geq 2\tau(K)$. }\end{cases}

We also study the effect of L-space satellite operators on Hom’s
\varepsilon
-invariant. We describe conditions under which we can guarantee that
\varepsilon(P(K,n))>-1
. Note that
\varepsilon(P(K,n))=0
implies
\tau(P(K,n))=0
, so if
\tau(P(K,n))\neq 0
, and the criteria below are satisfied, then we can conclude
\varepsilon(P(K,n))=1
. When
\tau(P(K,n))=0
, a more detailed cases-by-case analysis is needed.

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
P
be an L-space satellite operator, and
g_{3}(P)
denote the Seifert genus of the pattern knot
P
. Orient the corresponding two-component link
L_{P}
such that the linking number
\ell\geq 0
. Then, we have the following inequality of
\varepsilon(P(K,n))
:

(1)

If
\varepsilon(K)=1
, or
\varepsilon(K)=0
and
n\geq 0
, then

\varepsilon(P(K,n))\neq-1.

(2)

Suppose further that the H-function of the two-component link
L_{P}
satisfies

(1.3)

R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}\geq g_{3}(P)+\frac{\ell}{2}.

Then, if
\varepsilon(K)=0
and
n<0
, or
\varepsilon(K)=-1
, we have

\varepsilon(P(K,n))\neq-1.

In particular, if the condition in Equation (
1.3
) is satisfied, then for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the map on the smooth concordance group

\displaystyle P:\mathscr{C}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathscr{C}

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle\longmapsto P(K,n)

is not surjective. This extends the non-surjectivity results in
[
22
]
and
[
38
]
.

1.3.
Slice genus bounds and L-space satellite operators

We use our bounds on
\tau
to investigate the slice genus of satellite knots. We recall that if
K\subseteq S^{3}
is a knot, the
slice genus
of
K
, denoted
g_{4}(K)
, is the minimum genus of a smoothly embedded, oriented surface in
B^{4}
which bounds
K
.

If
P\subseteq S^{1}\times D^{2}
is a satellite knot, then we write
g_{3}^{\rel}(P)
for the minimum genus of a surface in
S^{1}\times D^{2}
which has boundary equal to
P
and
|\ell|
copies of a 0-framed longitude of
S^{1}\times D^{2}
, where
\ell
is the winding number of
P
.

We prove the following result, which was suggested to us by Jennifer Hom:

Theorem 1.5
.

Let
K\subseteq S^{3}
be a knot such that
g_{4}(K)=\tau(K)>0
and let
P
be an L-space satellite operator. Write
\ell
for the linking number of
L_{P}
(recall that this is typically called the winding number of
P
). Then

g_{4}(P(K,0))=\tau(P(K,0))=g_{3}^{\rel}(P)+|\ell|g_{4}(K).

As an example, note that if
K
is itself an L-space knot, then
\tau(K)=g_{4}(K)
.

Our proof of Theorem
1.5
uses our formula for
\tau(P(K,0))
from Theorem
1.2
, together with the description of the Thurston norm of the link
L_{P}
in terms of the
H
-function of
L_{P}
, due to Liu
[
28
]
.

We are also able to prove an additional result for L-space satellites with winding number 0:

Proposition 1.6
.

Suppose that
K\subseteq S^{3}
is a knot with
g_{4}(K)=\tau(K)>0
, and
P
is an L-space satellite operator with winding number 0. Then

g_{4}(P(K,n))=g_{3}^{\rel}(P)

for all
n<2\tau(K)
.

To illustrate the above result, we consider the positively clasped Whitehead pattern
P=\mathrm{Wh}_{+}
. By
[
14
, Theorem 1.4]
, if
n<2\tau(K)
then
1=|\tau(\mathrm{Wh}_{+}(K,n))|\leq g_{4}(\mathrm{Wh}_{+}(K,n))\leq g_{3}(\mathrm{Wh}_{+}(K,n))=1,
so we obtain
g_{4}(\mathrm{Wh}_{+}(K,n))=1=g_{3}^{\rel}(\mathrm{Wh}_{+})
for any
K\subseteq S^{3}
. Therefore Proposition
1.6
is a natural generalization of Hedden’s result.

In Section
8.3
we introduce a family of L-space satellite operators
P_{r,q}
for odd integers
r
and
q
such that
r,q>1
with winding number
\frac{r-q}{2}
(see Figure
8.2
for an example). When
r=q
, this gives an infinite family of L-space satellite operators with winding number
0
, where
r=q=3
recovers the Whitehead double.

1.4.
Patterns with minimal wrapping number

Recall that the
wrapping number
of a pattern
P
is the minimal geometric intersection number between
P
and a meridianal disk in
S^{1}\times D^{2}
. By definition, the wrapping number is at least
|\ell|=|\mathrm{lk}(P,\mu)|
. Our formulas simplify somewhat when the wrapping number is equal to
|\ell|
. In this case, we will say that
P
has
minimal wrapping number
.

If
P
is braided about
\mu
(for example if
P
is a cabling operator) then
P
has minimal wrapping number.

We prove the following:

Proposition 1.7
.

Suppose that
P
is an L-space pattern with minimal wrapping number. Then

R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}-|\ell|/2=g_{3}^{\rel}(P)=g_{3}(P).

Note that Proposition
1.7
can be used to simplify some of the computations in Theorem
1.2
. In particular, if
\varepsilon(K)>0
and
P
is a minimally wrapped L-space satellite operator, then the first part of Theorem
1.2
now reads

\tau(P(K,n))=g_{3}(P)+\frac{(\ell-1)\ell}{2}n+\ell\tau(K),

whenever
\ell\geq 0
. This quickly recovers Hom’s formula
[
16
]
for the
\tau
invariant of a cable of a knot
K
when
\varepsilon(K)>0
. Using duality, one may derive the formula when
\varepsilon(K)<0
from the formula for
\varepsilon(K)>0
. See Section
8
for more details.

When
P
has minimal wrapping number, we can also extend Theorem
1.5
to handle the case that
n\geq 0
. We will prove the following:

Proposition 1.8
.

Suppose that
P
is an L-space satellite operator which has minimal wrapping number and let
n\geq 0
. Assume that
\ell=\mathrm{lk}(P,\mu)\geq 0
. If
\tau(K)=g_{4}(K)>0
, then

g_{4}(P(K,n))=\tau(P(K,n))=g_{3}(P)+\frac{\ell(\ell-1)}{2}n+\ell\tau(K).

Proof.

The statement is an immediate consequence of Theorems
1.5
and Proposition
1.7
.
∎

1.5.
L-space satellite operators and homomorphism obstructions

Using our computation of
\tau
from Theorem
1.2
, as well as our result from
[
5
]
that the
H
-function of
L_{P}
determines
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}(L_{P})
when
L_{P}
is an L-space link, we show that L-space satellite operators satisfy a conjecture by Hedden and Pinzón-Caicedo
[
13
]
about when satellite operators act by group homomorphisms on the concordance group. We let
\mathscr{C}
denote the smooth concordance group of knots in
S^{3}
. This group is generated by oriented knots, modulo smooth concordance. The group operation is connected sum.

Hedden and Pinzón-Caicedo conjecture that the only homomorphisms induced by satellite operations are the following:

(1)

The zero map.

(2)

The identity map.

(3)

The orientation reversal map (i.e. the map which sends
K
to itself with orientation reversed).

In
[
31
]
,
[
24
]
and
[
19
]
, the conjecture is proven for several families of patterns. In
[
31
]
and
[
24
]
the conjecture is proven for patterns satisfying certain technical conditions involving branched covers of the pattern, while in
[
19
]
it is proven for all patterns where the winding number is even, but not divisible by 8.

The signature function
\sigma_{K}(\omega)
of Levine-Tristram
[
23
]

[
42
]
also provides a simple homomorphism obstruction. We recall the formula of Levine-Tristram signature of satellite knots in
[
27
]
that

\sigma_{P(K,0)}(\omega)=\sigma_{P(U,0)}(\omega)+\sigma_{K}(\omega^{\ell}).

Therefore, if
\sigma_{P(U,0)}(\omega)
is non-zero on a set of positive measure, then
P
cannot act by a homomorphism on the concordance group.

Using our results about
\tau(P(K,n))
for L-space satellite operators, we prove the following:

Theorem 1.9
.

If
L_{P}=\mu\cup P
is an
L
-space link, and the map

\displaystyle P:\mathscr{C}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathscr{C}

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle\longmapsto P(K,n)

is a group homomorphism for some
n\geq 0
, then

L_{P}
is the
2
-component unlink, the positive Hopf link, or the negative Hopf link. In particular, the satellite operator
P
induces either the trivial map, the identity map, or the orientation-reversing map.

1.6.
Further applications

In Section
10
we prove an additional result, which is independent of our computation of
\tau
. We prove the following general inequality about the concordance invariants of L-space satellites:

Proposition 1.10
.

Suppose that
P
is an L-space satellite with linking number
\ell\geq 0
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. For any knot
K\subseteq S^{3}
, there are inequalities

(1)

\tau(P(K,n))\geq\tau(K_{\ell,\ell n+1})
.

(2)

V_{k}(P(K,n))\geq V_{k}(K_{\ell,\ell n+1})
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(3)

\Upsilon_{P(K,n)}(t)\leq\Upsilon_{K_{\ell,\ell n+1}}(t)
for all
t\in[0,2]
.

In the above,
V_{k}
denotes Rasmussen’s local
h
-invariant
[
40
]
, and
\Upsilon_{K}(t)
denotes the concordance invariant of Ozsváth, Stipsicz and Szabó
[
32
]
.

When
\ell=0
, we observe that the cabled knot
K_{\ell,\ell n+1}
is always the unknot, so we immediately obtain:

Corollary 1.11
.

If
P
is a winding number 0 L-space satellite operator, then

\tau(P(K,n))\geq 0,\quad\text{and}\quad\Upsilon_{P(K,n)}(t)\leq 0.

We show Proposition
1.10
, by showing that if
P
is an L-space satellite operator of winding number
\ell\geq 0
, then there exists a natural map of bimodules

{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L_{P})^{\mathbb{F}[W,Z]}\to{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(T(2,2\ell))^{\mathbb{F}[W,Z]}.

We study the above map to extract the inequalities in Proposition
1.10
.

1.7.
Organization

In Section
2
, we recall some background on knot and link Floer homology, in particular the notion of the
H
-function. In Section
3
, we review the notion of link surgery modules. In Section
4
, we study in detail the link surgery bimodules of the link
L_{P}
associated with an L-space satellite operator
P
. In Section
5
, we compute
\tau(P(K,n))
. In Section
6
, we discuss the inequality of the
\varepsilon
-invariant for
P(K,n)
. In Section
7
, we examine the relation between our formula with the Thurston norm and slice genus. In Section
8
, we perform some example computations of
\tau(P(K,n))
using our formula. In Section
9
, we prove the homomorphism obstruction result for L-space satellite operators. In Section
10
, we describe a local inequality of
P(K,n)
.

1.8.
Acknowledgements

We thank Jennifer Hom and Yi Ni for helpful discussions. DC was partially supported by the Simons Collaboration Grant on New Structures in Low Dimensional Topology. IZ was partially supported by NSF grants 2204375, 2513241 and a Sloan fellowship. HZ was supported by an AMS-Simons travel grant.

2.
Background on knot and link Floer homology

2.1.
Knot Floer homology

Knot Floer homology is a refinement of Heegaard Floer homology for knots, due independently to Ozsváth and Szabó
[
34
]
, and Rasmussen
[
39
]
. Link Floer homology is a generalization of this theory to links, due to Ozsváth and Szabó
[
35
]
.

Given a knot in
S^{3}
, we will consider a finitely generated, free chain complex
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(K)
over the two-variable polynomial ring

R=\mathbb{F}[W,Z].

The chain complex
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(K)
has a
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
Maslov bigrading, denoted
(\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}},\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})
. Here,

(\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}},\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})(W)=(-2,0)\quad\text{and}\quad(\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}},\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})(Z)=(0,-2).

The
Alexander grading
is defined to be

A:=\frac{\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}-\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}}{2}.

If
L\subseteq S^{3}
is an
n
-component link, we consider a version of link Floer homology, denoted
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}(L)
, which takes the form of a finitely generated, free chain complex
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}(L)
over the ring

\mathbb{F}[W_{1},Z_{1},\dots,W_{n},Z_{n}].

This chain complex has a
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
-valued Maslov bigrading
(\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}},\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})
and an
n
-component Alexander grading
A=(A_{1},\dots,A_{n})
. The Alexander grading takes values in the set

(2.1)

\mathbb{H}(L)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\mathbb{Z}+\frac{\mathrm{lk}(L_{i},L\setminus L_{i})}{2}\right),

where
L_{1},\dots,L_{n}
denote the components of
L
. The Alexander grading satisfies

\sum_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}=\frac{\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}-\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}}{2}.

The differential has the following grading:

(\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}},\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})(\partial)=(-1,-1)\quad\text{and}\quad A(\partial)=(0,\dots,0).

2.2.
Concordance invariants

We now recall the definitions of several knot Floer concordance invariants which appear in our paper. Firstly, we recall Ozsváth and Szabó’s
\tau(K)
-invariant
[
33
]
. Write
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}(K)
for
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(K)/(W)
, and write
\mathcal{H\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}(K)
for
H_{*}\left(\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}(K)\right)
. In the literature, this is frequently denoted
\mathit{HFK}^{-}(K)
. The localization
Z^{-1}\mathcal{H\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}(K)
can be identified with
\widehat{\mathit{HF}}(S^{3})\otimes\mathbb{F}[Z,Z^{-1}]
, and therefore we have

\mathcal{H\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}(K)\cong\mathbb{F}[Z]\oplus\Tors,

where
\Tors
is an
\mathbb{F}[Z]
-module which satisfies
Z^{N}\cdot\Tors=0
for sufficiently large
N
. The
\tau(K)
invariant is defined as the Alexander grading of the generator of the tower
\mathbb{F}[Z]
, or equivalently

\tau(K)=\min\{A(\bm{\mathbf{x}}):\bm{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathcal{H\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}(K),\,\bm{\mathbf{x}}\text{ is $Z$-non-torsion}\}.

We now recall Hom’s
\varepsilon
invariant
[
17
]
. Write
\hat{R}=\mathbb{F}[W,Z]/(U)
, where
U=WZ
. We consider knot Floer complexes over
\hat{R}
, defined by

\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}_{\hat{R}}(K):=\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(K)\otimes{}_{\mathbb{F}[W,Z]}\hat{R}.

Definition 2.1
.

We say a chain map
F\colon\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}_{\hat{R}}(K_{1})\to\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}_{\hat{R}}(K_{2})
is an
\hat{R}
-local map
if

(1)

The map
F
preserves the
\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
-grading and is homogeneous with respect to the
\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
-grading.

(2)

The map
F
map is an isomorphism on homology after quotienting by
\mathbb{F}[Z]
-torsion.

(This is called a
local map
in
[
8
]
*Definition 3.3).

Viewing
\hat{R}
as
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}_{\hat{R}}(U)
, where
U
is the unknot, we say that
\varepsilon(K)\in\{0,1\}
if there is an
\hat{R}
-local map from
\hat{R}
to
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}_{\hat{R}}(K)
. We say that
\varepsilon(K)\in\{0,-1\}
if there is an
\hat{R}
-local map from
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}_{\hat{R}}(K)
to
\hat{R}
. We say that
\varepsilon(K)=0
if and only if there are
\hat{R}
-local maps in both directions, and we set
\varepsilon(K)
to be
+1
or
-1
otherwise. For the equivalence with Hom’s original definition, see
[
8
]
*Remark 2.4.

2.3.
The
H
-function

The
H
-function of a link
L\subseteq S^{3}
takes the form of a map

H_{L}\colon\mathbb{H}(L)\to\mathbb{Z}^{\geq 0}.

To define
H_{L}
, we consider a subcomplex

\mathcal{C\!F\!L}(L,\bm{\mathbf{s}})\subseteq\mathcal{C\!F\!L}(L),

for each
\bm{\mathbf{s}}\in\mathbb{H}(L)
. The complex
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}(L,\bm{\mathbf{s}})
is the subspace in Alexander grading
\bm{\mathbf{s}}
. We write

U_{i}=W_{i}Z_{i},\text{ for }i=1,\dots,n,

and note that the actions of
U_{1},\dots,U_{n}
preserve the
n
-component Alexander grading. Since the differential also preserves the Alexander grading, the complex
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}(L,\bm{\mathbf{s}})
is a free, finitely generated chain complex over
\mathbb{F}[U_{1},\dots,U_{n}]
.

It is not hard to see that for all
i,j
, the variables
U_{i}
and
U_{j}
induce chain homotopic endomorphisms of
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}(L,\bm{\mathbf{s}})
. See, e.g.,
[
4
]
*Equation 2.8.
Therefore, we view the homology group
\mathcal{H\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptL}(L,\bm{\mathbf{s}})
as a module over
\mathbb{F}[U]
, where
U
acts by any of the
U_{i}
. By the link surgery formula by Manolescu and Ozsváth
[
30
]
, we have

\mathcal{H\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptL}(L,\bm{\mathbf{s}})\cong\mathbb{F}[U]\oplus\Tors

as
\mathbb{F}[U]
-modules, where
\Tors
is an
\mathbb{F}[U]
-module which satisfies
U^{N}\cdot\Tors=0
for sufficiently large
N
. (i.e., the
U
-torsion part.)

We make the following definition (which first appeared in
[
10
]
):

Definition 2.2
.

The map
H_{L}\colon\mathbb{H}(L)\to\mathbb{Z}
is defined via the equation

H_{L}(\bm{\mathbf{s}})=-\frac{1}{2}\max\{\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}):\bm{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathcal{H\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptL}(L,\bm{\mathbf{s}}),\bm{\mathbf{x}}\text{ is $U$-non-torsion}\}.

We now recall the definition of an
L
-space link:

Definition 2.3
.

If
L\subseteq S^{3}
is a link, we say that
L
is an
L-space link
if
S^{3}_{\Lambda}(L)
is an L-space for all sufficiently positive framings
\Lambda
.

The following is a helpful reformulation of the above definition. See, e.g.,
[
29
]
*Proposition 1.11:

Lemma 2.4
.

A link
L\subseteq S^{3}
is an L-space link if and only if
\mathcal{H\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptL}(L,\bm{\mathbf{s}})\cong\mathbb{F}[U]
for all
\bm{\mathbf{s}}\in\mathbb{H}(L)
. Equivalently,
L
is an L-space link if and only
\mathcal{H\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptL}(L)
is free as an
\mathbb{F}[U]
-module.

The following result, due to Gorsky and Némethi, describes how to compute the
H
-function of an L-space link in terms of the multivariable Alexander polynomials of
L
and its sublinks:

Proposition 2.5

(
[
10
]
*Theorem 2.10)
.

Let
L
be an oriented L-space link in
S^{3}
. Then

H_{L}(\bm{\mathbf{s}})=\sum_{L^{\prime}\subseteq L}(-1)^{|L^{\prime}|-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{\mathbf{s}}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{H}(L^{\prime})\\
\bm{\mathbf{s}}^{\prime}\geq\pi_{L,L^{\prime}}(\bm{\mathbf{s}}+\bm{\mathbf{1}})\end{subarray}}\chi(\mathit{HFL}^{-}(L^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{s}}^{\prime})).

In the above,
\mathit{HFL}^{-}(L^{\prime})
denotes the homology of
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}(L^{\prime})/(Z_{i}:L_{i}\subseteq L^{\prime})
. Furthermore,
\pi_{L,L^{\prime}}\colon\mathbb{H}(L)\to\mathbb{H}(L^{\prime})
is the function which omits each component associated to link components of
L\setminus L^{\prime}
, and which modifies the
i
-th component (whenever
L_{i}\subseteq L^{\prime}
) via the transformation

s_{i}\mapsto s_{i}-\frac{\mathrm{lk}(L_{i},L\setminus L^{\prime})}{2}.

Also,
\bm{\mathbf{1}}
denotes
(1,\dots,1)
.

By
[
35
]
*Section 1.1,
\chi(\mathit{HFL}^{-}(L,\bm{\mathbf{s}}))
is the coefficient of the monomial
x_{1}^{s_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{s_{n}}
in a suitable normalization of the multivariable Alexander polynomial. More explicitly, Let
{\Delta}_{L}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
be the symmetric multivariable Alexander polynomial of
L
. Let

(2.2)

\widetilde{\Delta}_{L}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}):=\begin{cases}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})^{\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{L}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})&\text{if $n>1$}\\
\Delta_{L}(x)/(1-x^{-1})&\text{if $n=1$}\\
\end{cases}

denote the normalized multivariable Alexander polynomial, which satisfies the relation

\widetilde{\Delta}_{L}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{H}(L)}\chi(\mathit{HFL}^{-}(L,\mathbf{s}))x_{1}^{s_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{s_{n}},

where
\mathbf{s}=(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})
. For
n=1
, we expand
1/(1-x^{-1})
as a power series in
x^{-1}
. There is an overall sign ambiguity of
\widetilde{\Delta}_{L}
. For an L-space link
L
, this indeterminacy can be eliminated by assuming the
H
-function takes non-negative value, and differs by at most
1
when moving by the unit vector in each direction.

In this paper, we focus mostly on 2-component L-space links
L=L_{1}\cup L_{2}
. We will typically write
(t,r)\in\mathbb{H}(L)
for the Alexander gradings, where
t
is the one associated to the component
L_{1}
, and
r
is the one associated to the component
L_{2}
. Additionally, we will be most interested in two-component
L
-space links of the form
L_{P}=\mu\cup P
, for a 2-component L-space satellite operator
P
. We use the convention that
L_{1}=\mu
and
L_{2}=P
.

We now recall several basic properties of the
H
-function
H_{L}(t,r)
for a two-component link
L=L_{1}\cup L_{2}
.

Lemma 2.6
.

Suppose that
L=L_{1}\cup L_{2}
is a two-component link. Write
\ell=\mathrm{lk}(L_{1},L_{2})
for the linking number of
L
, and write
H_{L_{1}}
and
H_{L_{2}}
for the
H
-functions of
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
respectively. Then the
H
-function
H_{L}(t,r)
satisfies the following properties:

(1)

(Non-negativity)

H_{L}(t,r)\geq 0.

(2)

(Monotonicity)

H_{L}(t,r)\geq H_{L}(t+1,r),\,\,\,H_{L}(t,r)\geq H_{L}(t,r+1).

(3)

(Bounded gap)

H_{L}(t,r)\leq H_{L}(t+1,r)+1,\,\,\,H_{L}(t,r)\leq H_{L}(t,r+1)+1.

(4)

(Stabilization)

\begin{split}H_{L_{1}}(t-\ell/2)&=H_{L}(t,\infty):=\lim_{r\to\infty}H_{L}(t,r)\\
H_{L_{2}}(r-\ell/2)&=H_{L}(\infty,r):=\lim_{t\to\infty}H_{L}(t,r).\end{split}

(5)

(Symmetry)

H_{L}(t,r)+t+r=H_{L}(-t,-r).

Most of the statements in Lemma
2.6
follow directly from the definition of the
H
-function. Proofs of the above results can be found in, e.g.,
[
2
]
. Similar properties also hold for links with more components with appropriate changes.

Remark 2.7
.

The “bounded gap” property is equivalent to the monotonicity of a version of the
H
-function which is defined using the grading
\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
instead of
\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
. We denote this function

\bar{H}_{L}(t,r):=-\frac{1}{2}\max\{\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}):\bm{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathcal{H\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptL}(L,t,r),\bm{\mathbf{x}}\text{ is $U$-non-torsion}\}

and note that the symmetry of knot Floer homology implies that

\bar{H}_{L}(t,r)=H_{L}(t,r)+t+r.

Using this symmetry, we also obtain a stabilization result of the
H
-function as
t,r\to-\infty
in the following sense:

\bar{H}_{L_{1}}(t+\ell/2)=\lim_{r\to-\infty}\bar{H}_{L}(t,r),\,\,\bar{H}_{L_{2}}(r+\ell/2)=\lim_{t\to-\infty}\bar{H}_{L}(t,r).

Example 2.8
.

In Figure
2.1
, we show the
H
-function of the two component unlink
U_{2}
, the positive Hopf link
\mathcal{H}_{+}
(with linking number
1
), and the negative Hopf link
\mathcal{H}_{-}
(with linking number
-1
). These will be important in Section
9
when we prove Theorem
1.9
. They have the normalized multivariable Alexander polynomials, normalized as in Equation (
2.2
).

\widetilde{\Delta}_{U_{2}}(x_{1},x_{2})=0,\quad\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathcal{H}_{+}}(x_{1},x_{2})=x_{1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}x_{2}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}},\quad\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathcal{H}_{-}}(x_{1},x_{2})=-x_{1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}x_{2}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}.

\hline\cr\vdots&\ddots&2&1&0&0&0&0&\\

Figure 2.1.
The
H
-functions of the 2-component unlink
U_{2}
, the positive Hopf link
\mathcal{H}_{+}
and the negative Hopf link
\mathcal{H}_{-}
.

3.
Background on link surgery modules

In this section, we recall some background on the knot and link surgery modules described in
[
44
,
45
]
.

3.1.
The surgery algebra

We now recall the surgery algebra from
[
44
]
.

Definition 3.1
.

The
surgery algebra

\mathcal{K}
is an associative algebra over the ring of two idempotents
\bm{\mathbf{I}}=\bm{\mathbf{I}}_{0}\oplus\bm{\mathbf{I}}_{1}
, where each
\bm{\mathbf{I}}_{i}=\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{Z}/2
. Furthermore

\bm{\mathbf{I}}_{0}\cdot\mathcal{K}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{I}}_{0}=\mathbb{F}[W,Z],\quad\bm{\mathbf{I}}_{0}\cdot\mathcal{K}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{I}}_{1}=0

\bm{\mathbf{I}}_{1}\cdot\mathcal{K}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{I}}_{0}=\mathbb{F}[U,T,T^{-1}]\otimes\langle\sigma,\tau\rangle\quad\text{and},\quad\bm{\mathbf{I}}_{1}\cdot\mathcal{K}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{I}}_{1}=\mathbb{F}[U,T,T^{-1}].

The algebra is subject to the relations that

\sigma W=UT^{-1}\sigma,\quad\sigma Z=T\sigma,\quad\tau W=T^{-1}\tau,\quad\tau Z=UT\tau.

3.2.
Type-
D
,
A
,
DA
and
AA
-modules

In this section, we briefly recall the formalism of type-
D
,
A
and
DA
modules of Lipshitz, Ozsváth and Thurston
[
26
]
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an associative algebra over a ring
\bm{\mathbf{k}}
. A
right type-
D
module
, denoted
\mathcal{X}^{\mathcal{A}}=(\mathcal{X},\delta^{1})
, consists of a right
\bm{\mathbf{k}}
-module, equipped with a
\bm{\mathbf{k}}
-linear map

\delta^{1}\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}\otimes_{\bm{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{A}

which satisfies the equation

(\mathbb{I}_{\mathcal{X}}\otimes\mu_{2})\circ(\delta^{1}\otimes\mathbb{I}_{\mathcal{A}})\circ\delta^{1}=0,

where
\mu_{2}
denotes the multiplication action on
\mathcal{A}
.

A
left type-
A
module
over
\mathcal{A}
, denoted
{}_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{X}=(\mathcal{X},m_{j+1})
, is the same as a left
A_{\infty}
-module over
\mathcal{A}
. That is,
\mathcal{X}
is a left
\bm{\mathbf{k}}
-module and

m_{j+1}\colon\underbrace{\mathcal{A}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{A}}_{j}\otimes\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}

is a collection of
\bm{\mathbf{k}}
-linear maps, ranging over
j\geq 0
, such that

\sum_{i=0}^{n}m_{n-i}(a_{n},\dots a_{i+1},m_{i+1}(a_{i},\dots,a_{1},\bm{\mathbf{x}}))+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}m(a_{n},\dots,a_{i+1}a_{i},\dots,a_{1},\bm{\mathbf{x}})=0.

Finally, if
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are associative algebras over
\bm{\mathbf{k}}
and
\bm{\mathbf{j}}
, respectively, then a
DA
-bimodule

{}_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{X}^{\mathcal{B}}
consists of a
(\bm{\mathbf{k}},\bm{\mathbf{j}})
-bimodule
\mathcal{X}
, equipped with
(\bm{\mathbf{k}},\bm{\mathbf{j}})
-linear maps

\delta_{j+1}^{1}\colon\underbrace{\mathcal{A}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{A}}_{j}\otimes\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}\otimes\mathcal{B}.

These maps are required to satisfy the following associativity relation:

\begin{split}0=&\sum_{i=0}^{n}\bigg((\mathbb{I}_{\mathcal{X}}\otimes\mu_{2})\circ(\delta^{1}_{n-i}\otimes\mathbb{I}_{\mathcal{B}})(a_{n},\dots a_{i+1},-)\bigg)\bigg(\delta_{i+1}^{1}(a_{i},\cdots a_{1},\bm{\mathbf{x}})\bigg)\\
+&\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\delta_{n}^{1}(a_{n},\dots,a_{i+1}a_{i},\dots,a_{1},\bm{\mathbf{x}}).\end{split}

We refer the reader to
[
26
]
and
[
25
]
for more background on the categories of these modules and bimodules.

If
\mathcal{X}^{\mathcal{A}}
and
{}_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{Y}
are type-
D
and type-
A
modules, then Lipshitz, Ozsváth and Thurston
[
26
]
*Section 2.4 define a model for the derived tensor product of
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
, called the
box tensor product
, denoted

\mathcal{X}^{\mathcal{A}}\boxtimes{}_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{Y}.

If
\mathcal{A}
is an algebra over
\bm{\mathbf{i}}
, then the underlying group of the box tensor product is
\mathcal{X}\otimes_{\bm{\mathbf{i}}}\mathcal{Y}
. The differential is given by

\partial^{\boxtimes}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}\otimes\bm{\mathbf{y}})=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathcal{X}}\otimes m_{k+1})(\delta^{k}(\bm{\mathbf{x}})\otimes\bm{\mathbf{y}})

where
\delta^{k}\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}\otimes\mathcal{A}^{\otimes k}
is obtained by iterating
\delta^{1}

k
-times.

Finally we discuss
AA
-bimodules. If
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are associative algebras over
\bm{\mathbf{i}}
and
\bm{\mathbf{j}}
, an
AA
-bimodule

{}_{\mathcal{A}}M_{\mathcal{B}}
consists of a
(\bm{\mathbf{i}},\bm{\mathbf{j}})
-module
M
, equipped with structure maps

m_{i|1|j}\colon\mathcal{A}^{\otimes i}\otimes M\otimes\mathcal{B}^{\otimes j}\to M

which satisfy the following
A_{\infty}
-associativity relation:

\begin{split}0=&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq i\leq n\\
0\leq j\leq m\end{subarray}}m_{n-i|1|m-j}(a_{n},\dots,m_{i|1|j}(a_{i},\dots,a_{1},\bm{\mathbf{x}},b_{1},\dots,b_{j}),\dots,b_{m})\\
+&\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}m_{n-1|1|m}(a_{n},\dots,a_{i+1}a_{i},\dots,a_{1},\bm{\mathbf{x}},b_{1},\dots,b_{m})\\
+&\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}m_{n|1|m-1}(a_{n},\dots,a_{1},\bm{\mathbf{x}},b_{1},\dots,b_{i}b_{i+1},\dots,b_{m})\end{split}.

See
[
25
]
*Defintion 2.2.38 for more on type-
AA
bimodules.

We will also need a version of the homological perturbation lemma for
AA
-bimodules. We first recall that if
Z
and
M
are chain complexes, then we say that a diagram of maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}

is a
strong deformation retraction
of chain complexes from
M
onto
Z
if

\partial(\Pi)=0,\quad\partial(I)=0,\quad\Pi\circ I=\mathbb{I}_{Z},\quad I\circ\Pi=\mathbb{I}_{M}+\partial(H),

\Pi\circ H=0,\quad H\circ I=0,\quad\text{and}\quad H\circ H=0.

Here, we are writing
\partial(f)
for the morphism differential, i.e.
\partial(f)=f\circ\partial+\partial\circ f
. In particular,
\partial(\Pi)=0
is equivalent to
\Pi
being a chain map.

We now state the homological perturbation lemma for
AA
-bimodules.

Lemma 3.2
.

Suppose that
{}_{\mathcal{A}}M_{\mathcal{B}}
is a
AA
-bimodule and that
(Z,d)
is a chain complex. Suppose that the diagram of maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}

giving a strong deformation retraction of chain complexes from
M
onto
Z
. Then
Z
admits the structure of an
AA
-bimodule, denoted
{}_{\mathcal{A}}Z_{\mathcal{B}}
, so that
m_{0|1|0}=d
, and so that
{}_{\mathcal{A}}Z_{\mathcal{B}}\simeq{}_{\mathcal{A}}M_{\mathcal{B}}
.

The proof of the above is standard, see, e.g.,
[
37
]
*Lemma 2.11. The structure maps
m^{Z}_{i|1|j}
on
{}_{\mathcal{A}}Z_{\mathcal{B}}
have a convenient description. If
i+j>0
,
\bm{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{A}^{\otimes i}
,
\bm{\mathbf{b}}\in\mathcal{B}^{\otimes j}
and
\bm{\mathbf{x}}\in M
, then the structure map is given by a diagram of the following form:

(3.1)

In the above,
\Delta
is the map which splits
\mathcal{A}^{\otimes i}
and
\mathcal{B}^{\otimes j}
into its tensor factors. The above sum ranges over configurations where each
m
-labeled vertex has at least two inputs (i.e.
m_{0|1|0}
is never applied).

3.3.
Modules over the surgery algebra

We now recall some basic constructions from
[
44
]
. If
L\subseteq Y
is an
n
-component link with Morse framing
\Lambda
, then there is a type-
D
module

\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda}(L)^{\otimes^{n}\mathcal{K}}.

This type-
D
module encodes the data of link surgery complex for
L
. It is sometimes helpful also to turn some of the type-
D
actions into type-
A
actions. This is achieved by taking a suitable tensor product with an algebraically defined module
{}_{\mathcal{K}\otimes\mathcal{K}}[\mathbb{I}^{\Supset}]
, defined in
[
44
]
*Section 8.4.

Of particular importance for our purpose are the cases that
L
is a knot or a two-component link in
S^{3}
. When
K\subseteq S^{3}
is a knot and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, there is a type-
D
module

\mathcal{X}_{n}(K)^{\mathcal{K}}.

When
L
is a two-component link and
\Lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, there is a type-
DA
bimodule

{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda}(L)^{\mathcal{K}}.

In
[
44
]
*Theorem 12.1, the second author proves that the box tensor product of two surgery modules is homotopy equivalent to the surgery complex of the connected sum of the corresponding links. As a special case, if
L=L_{1}\cup L_{2}\subseteq S^{3}
is a two-component link with integral framing
\Lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
and
K\subseteq S^{3}
is a knot with framing
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, then

\mathcal{X}_{n}(K)^{\mathcal{K}}\boxtimes{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}(L)^{\mathcal{K}}\simeq\mathcal{X}_{(\lambda_{1}+n,\lambda_{2})}(K\#L)^{\mathcal{K}}.

Here the connected sum is formed by joining
K
with
L_{1}
.

3.4.
Hat flavored modules

In this section, we describe the modules over the hat flavor of the surgery algebra
\hat{\mathcal{K}}:=\mathcal{K}/(U)
, focusing on the case of knots in
S^{3}
.

There is an algebra morphism
q_{U}\colon\mathcal{K}\to\mathcal{K}/(U)
induced by setting
U=0
, and therefore an induced
DA
-bimodule
{}_{\mathcal{K}}[q_{U}]^{\hat{\mathcal{K}}}
. See
[
25
]
*Definition 2.2.48 for the
DA
-bimodule induced by an algebra morphism. If
K\subseteq Y
is a null-homologous knot with integral framing
n
, the hat flavored modules are defined via a box tensor product

\mathcal{X}_{n}(Y,K)^{\hat{\mathcal{K}}}:=\mathcal{X}_{n}(Y,K)^{\mathcal{K}}\boxtimes{}_{\mathcal{K}}[q_{U}]^{\hat{\mathcal{K}}}.

It will be helpful to have some strong results about the structure of
\mathcal{X}_{n}(K)^{\hat{\mathcal{K}}}
when
K
is a knot in
S^{3}
. As a first step, we note the entire complex
\mathcal{X}_{n}(K)^{\hat{\mathcal{K}}}
is determined by the knot Floer complex
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(K)^{\hat{R}}
over
\hat{R}=\mathbb{F}[W,Z]/(U)
. To see this, we first observe that idempotent
0
of
\mathcal{X}_{n}(K)^{\hat{\mathcal{K}}}
is just
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(K)^{\hat{R}}
and idempotent
1
of the module consists of a single generator
\bm{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}
with vanishing
\delta^{1}
. The
\sigma
weighted terms of the differential
\delta^{1}
from idempotent
0
to idempotent
1
are induced by the
v
-map in the surgery formula. This map is induced by a homotopy equivalence from
Z^{-1}\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(K)
to
\mathbb{F}[T,T^{-1}]\otimes\widehat{\mathit{CF}}(S^{3})\simeq\mathbb{F}[T,T^{-1}]
. This homotopy can be normalized to preserve the Alexander grading, if we equip
T^{i}\in\mathbb{F}[T,T^{-1}]
with Alexander grading
i
. This homotopy equivalence intertwines the action of
Z
with
T
and intertwines
W
with
0
, by
[
44
]
*Lemma 6.7. Since
\mathbb{F}[T,T^{-1}]
admits no grading preserving automorphisms other than the identity map, the map
v
is therefore uniquely determined up to chain homotopy. Similarly, the
\tau
weighted terms of
\delta^{1}
from idempotent
0
to idempotent
1
are determined by a homotopy equivalence
h
from
W^{-1}\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(K)
to
\mathbb{F}[T,T^{-1}]
which intertwines
W
with
T^{-1}
and intertwines the
Z
with
0
. The map
h
shifts the Alexander grading upward by
n
. Such a map is uniquely determined up to chain homotopy. In particular, when
K\subseteq S^{3}
, the type-
D
module
\mathcal{X}_{n}(K)^{\hat{\mathcal{K}}}
is determined up to homotopy equivalence by
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(K)^{\hat{R}}
. See
[
15
]
*Section 5.3 for a similar statement.

We now recall the structure theorem of Dai, Hom, Stoffregen and Truong
[
8
]
for
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(K)^{\hat{R}}
, when
K\subseteq S^{3}
. We recall first their notion of a
standard complex
. If
m
is even and
b_{1},\dots,b_{m}
are non-zero integers, then they define a free chain complex
C(b_{1},\dots,b_{m})
over
\hat{R}
. We view this complex as a type-
D
module over
\hat{R}
. This complex has
m+1
generators, which we denote

\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{0},\dots,\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{m}.

The differential is as follows.

(1)

If
i
is odd and
b_{i}<0
, then
\partial
contains a component from
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{i-1}
to
W^{|b_{i}|}\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{i}
.

(2)

If
i
is odd and
b_{i}>0
, then
\partial
contains a component from
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{i}
to
W^{b_{i}}\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{i-1}
.

(3)

If
i
is even and
b_{i}<0
, then
\partial
contains a component from
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{i-1}
to
Z^{|b_{i}|}\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{i}
.

(4)

If
i
is even and
b_{i}>0
, then
\partial
contains a component from
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{i}
to
Z^{b_{i}}\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{i-1}
.

Schematically, we can depict the standard complex
C(b_{1},\dots,b_{m})
as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{m}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{|b_{1}|}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{|b_{2}|}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{|b_{m}|}}

The direction of each arrow is determined by the sign of
b_{i}
. If
b_{i}<0
, then the arrow points to the right. If
b_{i}>0
, then the arrow points to the left.

The gradings are normalized so that
\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{0})=0
and
\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{m})=0
. If
C(b_{1},\dots,b_{m})
is a direct summand of
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(K)^{\hat{R}}
for
K\subseteq S^{3}
, then
\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{0})=-2\tau(K)
, and
\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{m})=-2\tau(K)
. Here,
\tau(K)
denotes the invariant of Ozsváth and Szabó
[
33
]
, as recalled in Section
2.2
.

Dai, Hom, Stoffregen and Truong prove the following structural result for
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(K)^{\hat{R}}
:

Theorem 3.3

(
[
8
]
*Theorem 6.1, Corollary 6.2)
.

If
K
is a knot in
S^{3}
, then there are integers
b_{1},\dots,b_{m}
so that

\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(K)^{\hat{R}}\simeq C(b_{1},\dots,b_{m})\oplus A,

where
A
is a free complex over
\hat{R}
which becomes acyclic after inverting
W
, and after inverting
Z
.

Remark 3.4
.

By
[
8
]
*Remark 3.16, Proposition 4.10, if
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(K)^{\hat{R}}\simeq C(b_{0},...,b_{m})\oplus A
, then the
\varepsilon
-invariant of
K
, recalled in Section
2.2
, is given by the following:

\varepsilon(K)=\begin{cases}0,&\text{ if }m=0,\\
\sign(b_{0})=-\sign(b_{m}),&\text{ if }m>0.\\
\end{cases}

One easy corollary of Theorem
3.3
is a structural result for the modules
\mathcal{X}_{n}(K)^{\hat{\mathcal{K}}}
when
K\subseteq S^{3}
. Given non-zero integers
b_{1},\dots,b_{m}
, we can define a type-
D
module
\mathcal{X}_{n}(b_{1},\dots,b_{m})^{\hat{\mathcal{K}}}
as follows. In idempotent 0, we use the complex
C(b_{1},\dots,b_{m})
. In idempotent 1, we use a complex with one generator,
\bm{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}
, which has Maslov and Alexander grading
(\gr,A)(\bm{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})=(0,0)
. We add two components to
\delta^{1}
: a component of
\bm{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}\otimes T^{\tau(K)}\sigma
to
\delta^{1}(\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{0})
, and a component of
\bm{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}\otimes T^{n-\tau(K)}\tau
to
\delta^{1}(\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{m})
. Schematically, the complex
\mathcal{X}_{n}(b_{1},\dots,b_{m})^{\hat{\mathcal{K}}}
has the following form:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\tau(K)}\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{|b_{1}|}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{|b_{2}|}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{|b_{m}|}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{n-\tau(K)}\tau}

There are no components of
\delta^{1}
from
A
to
\bm{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}
, as it becomes acyclic after inverting
W
or
Z
.

As an immediate consequence of Theorem
3.3
, and the above description of how to compute
\mathcal{X}_{n}(K)^{\hat{\mathcal{K}}}
in terms of
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(K)^{\hat{R}}
, we obtain the following description of
\mathcal{X}_{n}(K)^{\hat{\mathcal{K}}}
for knots in
S^{3}
:

Theorem 3.5
.

Suppose that
K\subseteq S^{3}
and that
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(K)^{\hat{R}}
decomposes as

\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(K)^{\hat{R}}\simeq C(b_{1},\dots,b_{m})\oplus A,

as in the statement of Theorem
3.3
. Then

\mathcal{X}_{n}(K)^{\hat{\mathcal{K}}}\simeq\mathcal{X}_{n}(b_{1},\dots,b_{m})^{\hat{\mathcal{K}}}\oplus A,

where we view
A
as being supported in idempotent 0.

Remark 3.6
.

Sometimes in computations, we will find it easier to replace
\bm{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}
with
\bm{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}\otimes T^{\tau(K)}
, so that
\mathcal{X}_{n}(K)^{\hat{\mathcal{K}}}
has the following form:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{|b_{1}|}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{|b_{2}|}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{|b_{m}|}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{n-2\tau(K)}\tau}

4.
DA
bimodules for L-space satellite operators

If
P
is a satellite operator, we write
L_{P}:=\mu\cup P
for the corresponding two-component link in
S^{3}
. We typically abuse notation and view
P
as both a knot in the solid torus, as well as the knot in
S^{3}
obtained by embedding the solid torus
S^{1}\times D^{2}
into
S^{3}
as a neighborhood of the
0
-framed unknot. We write
\mu
for the meridian
\{pt\}\times\partial D^{2}
.

Definition 4.1
.

We say that
P
is an
L-space satellite operator
if
L_{P}\subseteq S^{3}
is a two-component L-space link, i.e.,
S^{3}_{\Lambda}(L_{P})
is an L-space for all integral framings
\Lambda\gg 0
.

In this section, we review the computation of the bimodules
{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L_{P})^{\mathbb{F}[W,Z]}
from
[
5
]
.
To study the
\tau
invariant of satellites, we consider a version of this bimodule obtained by setting
W=0
and changing the type-
D
action to a type-
A
action. We denote this type-
AA
bimodule by

{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L_{P})_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}.

This module admits a model which has only
m_{1|1|0}
and
m_{0|1|1}
non-zero, though is typically not free as an
\mathbb{F}[Z]
-module. We will write

{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}^{\diamond}(L_{P})_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}

for this small model of
{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L_{P})_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
.

Of particular importance will be a quotient
AA
-bimodule of
{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}^{\diamond}(L_{P})_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
, which we denote by

{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}^{\free}(L_{P})_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}.

This module is simpler than
{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}^{\diamond}(L_{P})_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
, and is always free as an
\mathbb{F}[Z]
-module.
In many cases it is sufficient for the purposes of computing
\tau
of a satellite knot.

4.1.
The bimodule
{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L_{P})^{\mathbb{F}[W,Z]}

In
[
5
]
, we described a method for computing the bimodule
{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L)^{\mathbb{F}[W,Z]}
when
L
is a two-component L-space link. Together with the gluing formulas from
[
44
]
, this gave a technique for computing the effect of L-space satellite operators on knot Floer homology.

First, we recall the definition of staircase complexes.

Definition 4.2
.

A
staircase
is a finitely generated free chain complex over
R=\mathbb{F}[W,Z]
with a free
R
-basis
\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0},\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{1},\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{2},\dots,\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{2m-1},\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{2m}
, with respect to which the differential takes the following form:

\partial(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{2i})=0,\quad\text{and}\quad\partial(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{2i+1})=\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{2i}\otimes W^{\alpha_{i}}+\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{2i+2}\otimes Z^{\beta_{i}},

for some
\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}>0
.

The
WZ=0
reduction of a staircase complex is a special case of the standard complex described in Section
3.4
, which takes the form
C(\alpha_{0},-\beta_{0},\alpha_{1},-\beta_{1},\dots,\alpha_{m-1},-\beta_{m-1})
in the notation used there.

We refer to the generator
\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}
in a staircase complex as the
top generator
, as it has the largest Alexander grading.

Definition 4.3
.

We say that a staircase complex
\mathcal{S}
has
standard gradings
if
\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0})=0
and
\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{2m})=0
. This is the case whenever
\mathcal{S}
is the knot Floer complex
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(K)
of an L-space knot
K
in
S^{3}
.

There is also another grading on a staircase complex, denoted
\gr_{\alg}
, which has the property that

\gr_{\alg}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{2i})=0,\qquad\gr_{\alg}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{2i+1})=1,

and
\gr_{\alg}(W)=\gr_{\alg}(Z)=0
. The grading
\gr_{\alg}
is obtained by viewing the staircase complex as a two-step free resolution of its homology.

When
L_{P}
is an L-space link, we can encode the data of
{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L_{P})^{\mathbb{F}[W,Z]}
in terms of the diagram of the form shown in Figure
4.1
. Therein,
t\in\mathbb{Z}+\mathrm{lk}(\mu,P)/2
. Each of the complexes
\mathcal{C}_{t}
and
\mathcal{S}
are staircase complexes. Each
T^{t}\mathcal{S}
denotes a copy of
\mathcal{S}
. The staircase complexes
\mathcal{C}_{t}
and
\mathcal{S}
are determined by the
H
-function of
L
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{t-2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{t-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{t}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{t+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{t+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{t-2}\mathcal{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{t-1}\mathcal{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{t}\mathcal{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{t+1}\mathcal{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{t+2}\mathcal{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z|L_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma|L_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau|L_{\tau}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{subarray}{c}(W,Z)|h_{W,Z}\\
(Z,W)|h_{Z,W}\end{subarray}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z|L_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W|L_{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma|L_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau|L_{\tau}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{subarray}{c}(W,Z)|h_{W,Z}\\
(Z,W)|h_{Z,W}\end{subarray}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z|L_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W|L_{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma|L_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau|L_{\tau}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{subarray}{c}(W,Z)|h_{W,Z}\\
(Z,W)|h_{Z,W}\end{subarray}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z|L_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W|L_{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma|L_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau|L_{\tau}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{subarray}{c}(W,Z)|h_{W,Z}\\
(Z,W)|h_{Z,W}\end{subarray}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W|L_{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma|L_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau|L_{\tau}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{subarray}{c}(W,Z)|h_{W,Z}\\
(Z,W)|h_{Z,W}\end{subarray}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T|1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U|U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T|1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{-1}|1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U|U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T|1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{-1}|1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U|U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T|1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{-1}|1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U|U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{-1}|1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U|U}

Figure 4.1.
A schematic encoding the
DA
-bimodule of an L-space link
L
. Additional data (not shown) necessary to encode the
DA
module are the actions
\delta_{3}^{1}(\sigma,W,-)
,
\delta_{3}^{1}(\sigma,Z,-)
,
\delta_{3}^{1}(\tau,W,-)
and
\delta_{3}^{1}(\tau,Z,-)
.

The diagram in Figure
4.1
displays some of the
\delta_{2}^{1}
and
\delta_{3}^{1}
actions on this bimodule. For example, the arrows labeled
Z|L_{Z}
indicate the action of
\delta_{2}^{1}(Z,-)
. We will write
L_{Z}
for the induced map from
\mathcal{C}_{t}
to
\mathcal{C}_{t+1}
. The arrows labeled
(W,Z)|h_{W,Z}
indicate the actions
\delta_{3}^{1}(W,Z,-)
. Additionally, there will typically be several more
\delta_{3}^{1}
actions which are not shown in the above diagram. These would take the form of extra arrows labeled as
(\sigma,W)|h_{\sigma,W}
,
(\sigma,Z)|h_{\sigma,Z}
,
(\tau,W)|h_{\tau,W}
and
(\tau,Z)|h_{\tau,Z}
.

The above data does not explicitly include a description of algebra elements which are not degree 1 monomials, e.g.,
\delta_{2}^{1}(WZ^{2},-)
. In the case of L-space links, the remaining actions on the bimodule
{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L_{P})^{R}
are determined (up to overall homotopy equivalence), by the above choices of maps
L_{W}
,
L_{Z}
,
L_{\sigma}
,
L_{\tau}
,
h_{W,Z}
,
h_{Z,W}
,
h_{\sigma,W}
,
h_{\sigma,Z}
,
h_{\tau,W},
and
h_{\tau,Z}
. Furthermore, it is possible to choose the resulting bimodule
{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L_{P})^{R}
so that the actions are
U
-equivariant. See
[
5
]
*Section 5.5 for further details.

The staircase
\mathcal{S}
can also be identified with the knot Floer complex
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(P)
of the pattern
P
viewed as a knot in
S^{3}
. Here,
\mathcal{S}
is equipped with standard
(\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}},\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})
bigradings, in the sense of Definition
4.3
. We denote the generators of
\mathcal{S}
by
\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0},\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{2m^{\prime}}
.

The staircase
\mathcal{C}_{t}
is determined by the
H
-function of
L
, in the following way. We consider the restriction of the
H
-function,
H_{L}(t,-)
. We think of this as a single column of the
H
-function, if we plot the function
H_{L}(t,r)
as in Figure
4.2
. For each
t
, there are finitely many
r
such that

H_{L}(t,r+1)=H_{L}(t,r),\,\,\text{ and }\,\,H_{L}(t,r-1)=H_{L}(t,r)+1.

Label these values of
r
by
r^{t}_{0},r_{1}^{t},\dots,r^{t}_{m_{t}}
. For each
i=0,\dots,m_{t}
, we add a generator
\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{2i}^{t}
to
\mathcal{C}_{t}
such that

\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{t}_{2i})=-2H_{L}(t,r^{t}_{i}).

The
\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
-grading on link Floer homology satisfies
\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}=\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}-2A_{1}-2A_{2}
, so we define

\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{t}_{2i})=\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{t}_{2i})-2(A_{1}+A_{2})=-2H_{L}(t,r^{t}_{i})-2t-2r^{t}_{i}.

For each
0\leq i<m
, we add a generator
\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{2i+1}^{t}
, which satisfies

\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{t}_{2i+1})=\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{t}_{2i+2})+1,\quad\text{and}\quad\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{t}_{2i+1})=\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{t}_{2i})+1.

The grading shifts of the actions
L_{a}
for
a=W,Z,\sigma,\tau
are given as follows. When viewed as maps with domain
\mathcal{C}_{t}
, they have the following gradings:

(4.1)

\begin{split}(\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}},\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})(L_{W})&=(-2,0)\\
(\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}},\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})(L_{Z})&=(0,-2)\\
(\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}},\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})(L_{\sigma})&=(0,2t+\ell)\\
(\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}},\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})(L_{\tau})&=(-2t+\ell,0).\end{split}

A helpful consequence of the above formulas is that

A(L_{\sigma}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}))=A_{2}(\bm{\mathbf{x}})-\ell/2\quad\text{and}\quad A(L_{\tau}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}))=A_{2}(\bm{\mathbf{x}})+\ell/2.

In the above,
\ell=\mathrm{lk}(\mu,P)
, and we are writing
A_{2}(\bm{\mathbf{x}})
for second component in the
\mathbb{H}(L_{P})
-valued Alexander grading. Here, we give
T^{t}\mathcal{S}
the
(\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}},\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})
grading obtained by identifying it with
\mathcal{S}\cong\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(P)
. Also
A
denotes the Alexander grading
\tfrac{1}{2}(\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}-\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})
.

The maps
L_{W}
,
L_{Z}
,
L_{\sigma}
and
L_{\tau}
are determined up to chain homotopy by the fact that they have the above grading, they are chain maps with respect to
\delta_{1}^{1}
, and they are non-zero after taking homology with respect to
\delta_{1}^{1}
.

Example 4.4

(The positively clasped Whitehead double)
.

\begin{array}[]{|c|cccccccc|}\hline\cr\hbox{\diagbox[]{{\shortstack[l]{$r$}}}{{\shortstack[r]{$t$}}}}&\cdots&-2&-1&0&1&2&\cdots&\\

-1&\cdots&3&2&\boxed{1}&1&1&\cdots&\\
\vdots&\reflectbox{$\ddots$}&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\\

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{0}_{0}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U|U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T|1}
