.

For simplicity, when
K=U
is an unknot, we write
\mathbb{X}(P,U,n)^{\mathbb{F}[W,Z]}
for the complexes described in Lemma
5.7
. We can, of course, consider versions
\mathbb{X}^{\diamond}(P,U,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
and
\mathbb{X}^{\free}(P,U,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
as before. We warn the reader that the above complexes are not merely a truncation of
\mathcal{X}_{n}(U)^{\mathcal{K}}\boxtimes{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{H}_{-}^{\mathcal{K}}\boxtimes{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L_{P})^{\mathbb{F}[W,Z]}
. Instead they are only homotopy equivalent. (In fact, they are more naturally a truncation of the tensor product
\mathcal{D}_{\pm 1/n}^{\mathcal{K}}\boxtimes{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L_{P})^{\mathbb{F}[W,Z]}
where
\mathcal{D}_{\pm 1/n}^{\mathcal{K}}
is the
\pm 1/n
framed solid torus module described in
[
44
]
*Section 18.2).

As before, we will identify a direct summand of
\mathbb{X}^{\diamond}(P,U,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
that supports the infinite
Z
-tower in homology. This direct summand again consists of free generators of
\mathcal{C}^{\diamond}_{t}
and
\mathcal{S}^{\diamond}
. When
n>0
, this works for all L-space satellite operators
P
. When
n<0
, we impose additional restrictions on
P
for the argument to work. This leads to the extra assumption stated in Equation (
1.2
) in Theorem
1.2
.

Definition 5.8
.

(1)

When
n>0
, we define
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}(P,U,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}\subseteq\mathbb{X}^{\diamond}(P,U,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
to be the free chain complex over
\mathbb{F}[Z]
spanned by
n
-copies of
\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}
(one in each copy of
\mathcal{C}^{\diamond}_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}
), and
n+1
copies of
\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\prime}
(one in each adjacent copy of
\mathcal{S}^{\diamond}
).

(2)

When
n<0
, we define

\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime}(P,U,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}\subseteq\mathbb{X}^{\diamond}(P,U,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}

to be the free chain complex over
\mathbb{F}[Z]
spanned by
n
copies of
\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}
(one in each copy of
\mathcal{C}_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}^{\diamond}
),
n+1
copies of
\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\prime}
(one in each adjacent copy of
\mathcal{S}^{\diamond}
), as well as one copy of the generators
\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}
and
\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}
in the neighboring copies of
\mathcal{C}^{\diamond}_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\diamond}_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}
.

These complexes are shown in Figure
5.5
.

.

Lemma 5.10
.

Suppose that
P
is an L-space satellite operator. Orient
P
so the linking number
\ell
of the corresponding two-component link
L_{P}=\mu\cup P
is nonnegative. Suppose further that

R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}\geq g_{3}(P)+\frac{\ell}{2}-1.

Then the actions of
{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}^{\diamond}(L_{P})_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
satisfy

L_{\sigma}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\ell/2-1})=0,\quad L_{\tau}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\ell/2+1})=0.

Proof.

By the computation in the proof of Lemma
4.17
, we have

\displaystyle\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(L_{\sigma}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}))=-2,\quad

\displaystyle\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(L_{\sigma}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}))=-2-2R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}+\ell

and

\displaystyle\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0})=0,\quad

\displaystyle\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0})=-2g_{3}(P).

Therefore, when
\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0})\geq\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(L_{\sigma}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1})),
i.e., when

R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}\geq g_{3}(P)+\frac{\ell}{2}-1,

we may choose the action
L_{\sigma}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1})
on the full module
{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L_{P})^{\mathbb{F}[W,Z]}
so that

L_{\sigma}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1})=\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0}\otimes WZ^{R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}-\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-g_{3}(P)+1}=0\text{ in }\mathcal{S}\otimes\mathbb{F}[Z].

This implies that
L_{\sigma}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1})
will vanish in
\mathcal{X}^{\diamond}(L_{P})
.
For
L_{\tau}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}_{0})
, by similar computation, we may choose the action
L_{\tau}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}_{0})
on the full bimodule
{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L_{P})^{\mathbb{F}[W,Z]}
to vanish once we set
W=0
if

R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}\geq g_{3}(P)-\frac{\ell}{2}-1.

However, by Lemma
4.9
, we have

R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}\geq g_{3}(P)+\frac{\ell}{2}\geq g_{3}(P)-\frac{\ell}{2}>g_{3}(P)-\frac{\ell}{2}-1,

assuming
\ell\geq 0
.
∎

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
U
be the unknot, and suppose
P
is an L-space satellite operator. Then:

(1)

If
n>0
,
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}(P,U,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
is a direct summand of
\mathbb{X}^{\diamond}(P,U,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
.

(2)

If
n<0
and additionally

\ell\geq 0\quad\text{and}\quad R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}\geq g_{3}(P)+\frac{\ell}{2}-1,

then,
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime}(P,U,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
is a direct summand of
\mathbb{X}^{\diamond}(P,U,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
.
Here
\ell=\mathrm{lk}(\mu,P)
is the linking number of
L_{P}
, and
g_{3}(P)
is the Seifert genus of
P
viewed as a knot in
S^{3}
.

Proof.

We prove the case
n>0
first. From the description of
L_{\sigma}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}_{0})
,
L_{\tau}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}_{0})
from Lemma
4.17
and the shape of
\mathbb{X}^{\diamond}(P,U,n)
, it is easy to see that
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}(P,U,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
is a subcomplex of
\mathbb{X}^{\diamond}(P,K,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}.

To see that it is also a quotient complex, note that by Lemma
4.12
, there is a quotient complex
\mathbb{X}^{\free}(P,U,n)
of
\mathbb{X}^{\diamond}(P,U,n),
consisting of the generators
\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{t}_{0}
or
\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0}
in each copy of
\mathcal{C}_{t}^{\diamond}
or
\mathcal{S}^{\diamond}
. The complex
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}(P,U,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
is contained in
\mathbb{X}^{\free}(P,U,n)
, so it suffices to show
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}(P,U,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
is a quotient complex of
\mathbb{X}^{\free}(P,U,n)
. From the shape of the complex, the only generators in
\mathbb{X}^{\free}(P,U,n)
for which the differential could have a non-trivial component in
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}(P,U,n)
are the generators
\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}
in the rightmost copy of
\mathcal{C}_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}^{\diamond}
, and
\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}
in the leftmost copy of
\mathcal{C}_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}^{\diamond}.
Therefore it suffices to show the following:

(1)

The
\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\prime}
component of
L_{\sigma}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1})
vanishes.

(2)

The
\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\prime}
component of
L_{\tau}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1})
vanishes.

These two components vanish by Lemma
4.17
, which finishes the proof when
n>0
.

When
n<0
, it is easy to see
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime}(P,U,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
is a quotient complex of
\mathbb{X}^{\free}(P,U,n)
from the shape of the complex. Therefore it is also a quotient complex of
\mathbb{X}^{\diamond}(P,U,n).

To show that
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime}(P,U,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
is a subcomplex of
\mathbb{X}^{\diamond}(P,U,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]},
it suffices to show the following:

(1)

L_{\sigma}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1})=0
.

(2)

L_{\tau}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1})=0
.

This holds by Lemma
5.10
.
∎

Theorem 5.12
.

Suppose
K
is a knot in
S^{3}
with
\varepsilon(K)=0
and
P
is an L-space satellite operator. Let
\ell\geq 0
be the non-negative linking number
\ell=\mathrm{lk}(\mu,P)
of the two components of
L_{P}
. Then, we have the following formula for
\tau(P(K,n))
:

(1)

When
n\geq 0
,

\tau(P(K,n))=g+\frac{\ell(\ell-1)}{2}n.

(2)

When
n<0
, and if
P
satisfies

R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}\geq g+\frac{\ell}{2}-1,

then,

\tau(P(K,n))=\max\left\{R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}+\ell/2,R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}-\ell/2\right\}+\frac{\ell(\ell-1)}{2}n.

In the above,
g=g_{3}(P)
is the Seifert genus of
P
viewed as a knot in
S^{3}
, and
R_{t}
is the second Alexander grading in
\mathbb{H}(L_{P})
of the top generator
\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{t}_{0}
in the staircase
\mathcal{C}_{t}
, for
t=\frac{\ell}{2}-1,\frac{\ell}{2}
.

Proof.

As mentioned at the beginning of the section, when
\varepsilon(K)=0
,
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(P(K,n))
is locally equivalent to
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(P(U,n))
, so it is enough to compute
\tau(P(U,n))
.

The statement when
n=0
is trivial, since

\tau(P(U,0))=\tau(P)=g_{3}(P),

where the second quality holds because
P
is an L-space knot by the assumption that
L_{P}
is an L-space link.

When
n>0
, recall from Lemma
4.17
, that

L_{\sigma}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}_{0})=\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0}\cdot Z^{R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}-\frac{\ell}{2}-g_{3}(P)},\qquad L_{\tau}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}_{0})=\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0}\cdot Z^{R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}+\frac{\ell}{2}-g_{3}(P)}.

It is then easy to see that the homology of
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}(P(U,n))
contains an infinite
Z
-tower, which is an infinite
Z
-tower in
\mathcal{H\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}(P(U,n))
. Assuming
\ell\geq 0
, the generator of the
Z
-tower is the leftmost
\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0}
.

Recall the truncation and homotopy equivalence in
[
5
]
*Section 11.1 to obtain the model of
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(P(U,n))
in Lemma
5.7
from the larger complex
\mathcal{X}_{n}(U)^{\mathcal{K}}\boxtimes{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{H}_{-}^{\mathcal{K}}\boxtimes{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L_{P})^{\mathbb{F}[W,Z]}
. The leftmost
\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0}
in
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}(P(U,n))
lies in the copy
\mathcal{S}=F_{-\frac{1}{2},\frac{\ell-1}{2}}\subseteq\mathcal{X}_{n}(U)^{\mathcal{K}}\boxtimes{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{H}_{-}^{\mathcal{K}}\boxtimes{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L_{P})^{\mathbb{F}[W,Z]}
by the truncation procedure. Therefore, by the same computation as in the case
n\geq 2\tau(K)
in Theorem
5.6
(with
\tau(K)=0
), we get

\tau(P(U,n))=g_{3}(P)+\frac{\ell(\ell-1)}{2}n.

When
n<0
, again from Lemma
4.17
, we have:

L_{\sigma}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}_{0})=\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0}\cdot Z^{R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}-\frac{\ell}{2}-g_{3}(P)},\qquad L_{\tau}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}_{0})=\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0}\cdot Z^{R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}+\frac{\ell}{2}-g_{3}(P)}.

Therefore, the homology of
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime}(P,U,n)
again contains an infinite
Z
-tower of
\mathcal{H\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}(P(U,n))
. Assuming
\ell\geq 0
, the generator of the infinite
Z
-tower depends on the comparison between
R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}+\frac{\ell}{2}
and
R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}-\frac{\ell}{2}
.

(1)

If
R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}+\frac{\ell}{2}\geq R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}-\frac{\ell}{2}
, the generator of the infinite
Z
-tower is an appropriate linear combination of the generators in the top layer of
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime}(P,U,n)
, which has the same Alexander grading as the leftmost generator
\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}_{0}
. This copy of
\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}_{0}
lies in the copy of
\mathcal{C}_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}=J_{n+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}},\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}\subseteq\mathcal{X}_{n}(U)^{\mathcal{K}}\boxtimes{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{H}_{-}^{\mathcal{K}}\boxtimes{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L_{P})^{\mathbb{F}[W,Z]}
by the truncation procedure in
[
5
]
*Section 11.1. By the grading shift formula in
[
5
]
*Section 9.5, we have

\tau(P(U,n))=R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}+\left(n+\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\ell-3}{2}n\right)\ell=R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}+\frac{\ell}{2}+\frac{(\ell-1)\ell}{2}n.

(2)

If
R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}+\frac{\ell}{2}<R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}-\frac{\ell}{2}
, the generator of the infinite
Z
-tower has the same Alexander grading as
\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}_{0}\cdot Z^{R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}-R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}-\ell}
, where
\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}
is again the leftmost generator lying in the copy
\mathcal{C}_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}=J_{n+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}},\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}\subseteq\mathcal{X}_{n}(U)^{\mathcal{K}}\boxtimes{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{H}_{-}^{\mathcal{K}}\boxtimes{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L_{P})^{\mathbb{F}[W,Z]}
. Therefore,

\tau(P(U,n))=R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}+\frac{\ell}{2}+\frac{(\ell-1)\ell}{2}n+R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}-R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}-\ell=R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}-\frac{\ell}{2}+\frac{(\ell-1)\ell}{2}n.

∎

Remark 5.13
.

If
n<0
, and the condition

R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}\geq g+\frac{\ell}{2}-1,

does not hold, then the generator of the infinite
Z
-tower of
\mathbb{X}^{\diamond}(P,U,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
will typically involve generators of the staircases
\mathcal{C}_{t}^{\diamond}
and
\mathcal{S}^{\diamond}
other than the free generators. This appears to require a more careful case-by-case analysis depending on the pattern
P
.

5.5.
Formulas for
\tau(P(K,n))
when
\varepsilon(K)=-1

Now, suppose
\varepsilon(K)=-1
. Then, by
[
8
]
, in the decomposition

\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}_{\hat{R}}(K)\simeq C(b_{1},\dots,b_{m})\oplus A,

we have
b_{1}=-b_{m}<0
, so
C(b_{1},\dots,b_{m})
takes the form:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{m-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{m}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{-b_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{|b_{2}|}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{|b_{m-1}|}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{b_{m}}}

In particular, there are arrows leaving
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{0}
and
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{m}
in
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}_{\hat{R}}(K)
, which differs from the situation when
\varepsilon(K)=1
. Under the extra assumption

R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}\geq g+\frac{\ell}{2}-1

as stated in Equation (
1.2
) in Theorem
1.2
, we will identify a direct summand of
\mathbb{X}^{\diamond}(P,K,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
that supports the unique infinite
Z
-tower in the homology. This direct summand is a slight modification of the one defined in Section
5.2
, and we will use it to compute
\tau(P(K,n))
.

Definition 5.14
.

Define
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime\prime}(P,K,n)
to be the free chain complex over
\mathbb{F}[Z]
consisting of generators in
\mathfrak{C}(P,K,n)
, as in Definition
5.2
, together with the following four extra generators:

(1)

\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0}=\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}|\bm{\mathbf{f}}^{\free}_{-\frac{1}{2}+b_{1},\frac{\ell-1}{2}}\in\langle\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}\rangle\otimes\mathscr{F}^{\free}_{-\frac{1}{2}+b_{1},\frac{\ell-1}{2}}\subseteq F^{\free}_{\tau(K)-\frac{1}{2},\frac{\ell-1}{2}};

(2)

\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}_{0}=\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}|\bm{\mathbf{e}}^{\free}_{\frac{1}{2}+b_{1},\frac{\ell-1}{2}}\in\langle\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}\rangle\otimes\mathscr{E}^{\free}_{\frac{1}{2}+b_{1},\frac{\ell-1}{2}}\subseteq E^{\free}_{\tau(K)+\frac{1}{2},\frac{\ell-1}{2}};

(3)

\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{m-1}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0}=\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{m-1}|\bm{\mathbf{f}}^{\free}_{-\frac{1}{2}+b_{m},\frac{\ell+1}{2}}\in\langle\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{m-1}\rangle\otimes\mathscr{F}^{\free}_{-\frac{1}{2}+b_{m},\frac{\ell+1}{2}}\subseteq F^{\free}_{-\tau(K)-\frac{1}{2},\frac{\ell+1}{2}};

(4)

\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{m-1}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}_{0}=\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{m-1}|\bm{\mathbf{e}}^{\free}_{-\frac{1}{2}+b_{m},\frac{\ell+1}{2}}\in\langle\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{m-1}\rangle\otimes\mathscr{E}^{\free}_{-\frac{1}{2}+b_{m},\frac{\ell+1}{2}}\subseteq E^{\free}_{-\tau(K)-\frac{1}{2},\frac{\ell+1}{2}}.

The differentials in
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime\prime}(P,K,n)
are the components of the differential on
\mathbb{X}^{\free}(P,K,n)
between the generators of
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime\prime}(P,K,n)
.

, and its truncation.

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{\tau}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{Z}}

Figure 5.7.
The schematic drawing of the chain complex
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime\prime}(P,K,n)
and its truncation when
n>2\tau(K)
, with
n-2\tau(K)
copies of
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{0}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}_{0}
in the truncation.

Lemma 5.15
.

Suppose
K
is a knot in
S^{3}
with
\varepsilon(K)=-1
, and
P
is an L-space satellite operator satisfying

\ell\geq 0,\text{ and }\,R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}\geq g_{3}(P)+\frac{\ell}{2}-1.

Then,
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime\prime}(P,K,n)
is a direct summand of
\mathbb{X}^{\diamond}(P,K,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
.

Proof.

We will prove
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime\prime}(P,K,n)
is a quotient complex of
\mathbb{X}^{\free}(P,K,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
(hence a quotient complex of
\mathbb{X}^{\diamond}(P,K,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
as well), and a subcomplex of
\mathbb{X}^{\diamond}(P,K,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
. The proof follows the same approach as in Lemma
5.4
and Lemma
5.5
, so we will be brief, emphasizing only the parts that differ from the previous ones.

First, we prove
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime\prime}(P,K,n)
is a quotient complex of
\mathbb{X}^{\free}(P,K,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
, that is, there are no differentials from other generators in
\mathbb{X}^{\free}(P,K,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
to
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime\prime}(P,K,n)
. The row consisting of
J^{\free}_{*,\frac{\ell-1}{2}}
and
M_{*,\frac{\ell-1}{2}}^{\free}
is the same as the one in
\mathfrak{C}(P,K,n)
, and the same proof as before applies. We will focus on the row consisting of
E_{s,\frac{\ell-1}{2}}^{\free}
and
F_{s-1,\frac{\ell-1}{2}}^{\free}
for
s>\tau(K)
, and the result for the other row follows by similar argument.

As there is no length-
2
differential in
\mathbb{X}^{\free}(P,K,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
, it is enough to consider length-
0
and length-
1
differentials.

For length-
0
differentials, there are no differentials pointing to
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{0}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0}
or
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{0}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}_{0}
, since there are no differentials in
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}_{\hat{R}}(K)
pointing to
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{0}
when
\varepsilon(K)=-1
.

For
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}_{0}=\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}|\bm{\mathbf{e}}^{\free}_{\frac{1}{2}+b_{1},\frac{\ell-1}{2}}
in
E_{\tau(K)+\frac{1}{2},\frac{\ell-1}{2}},
there might be a non-trivial arrow

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{b_{2}}}

pointing to
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}
in
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}_{\hat{R}}(K)
, if
b_{2}
is positive. However, as shown in Figure
5.1
, all the
Z
-arrows pointing to
\mathscr{E}^{\free}_{s,t}
for
s<0
are trivial, and
\bm{\mathbf{e}}^{\free}_{\frac{1}{2}+b_{1},\frac{\ell-1}{2}}
lies in
\mathscr{E}^{\free}_{\frac{1}{2}+b_{1},\frac{\ell-1}{2}}
, with
\frac{1}{2}+b_{1}<0
by the assumption
\varepsilon(K)=-1
. Therefore, there is no length-
0
differential pointing to
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}_{0}
in
E^{\free}_{\tau(K)+\frac{1}{2},\frac{\ell-1}{2}}
either. The same argument applies to the potential length-
0
differential pointing to
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0}
in
F^{\free}_{\tau(K)-\frac{1}{2},\frac{\ell-1}{2}}
as well. Hence, there are no length-
0
differentials from other generators in
\mathbb{X}^{\free}(P,K,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
pointing to
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime\prime}(P,K,n)
.

For length-
1
differentials, the only difference from the situation in Lemma
5.4
is the map

\Phi_{\mu}:E^{\free}_{\tau(K)-\frac{1}{2},\frac{\ell-1}{2}}\to F^{\free}_{\tau(K)-\frac{1}{2},\frac{\ell-1}{2}}.

The component of
\Phi_{\mu}
pointing to
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{0}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0}
(resp.
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0}
) in
F^{\free}_{\tau(K)-\frac{1}{2},\frac{\ell-1}{2}}
is given by the component of
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{0}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\prime}
(resp.
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\prime}
) in
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{0}|L_{\sigma}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1})
(resp.
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}|L_{\sigma}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1})
).
However, by Lemma
4.17
, the
\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\prime}
component of
L_{\sigma}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1})
vanishes. Therefore, there is no length-
1
differential in
\mathbb{X}^{\free}(P,K,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
pointing to
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}(P(K,n))
either. This finishes the proof that
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime\prime}(P,K,n)
is a quotient complex of
\mathbb{X}^{\diamond}(P,K,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
.

Next, we show that
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime\prime}(P,K,n)
is also a subcomplex of
\mathbb{X}^{\diamond}(P,K,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
, that is, there is no differential from
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime\prime}(P,K,n)
to other generators in
\mathbb{X}^{\diamond}(P,K,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
.

For the row consisting of
J^{\diamond}_{*,\frac{\ell-1}{2}}
and
M^{\diamond}_{*,\frac{\ell-1}{2}}
, the same proof as in Lemma
5.5
applies, since this row is identical in
\mathfrak{C}(P,K,n)
and
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime\prime}(P,K,n).

We will prove the statement for the row consisting of
E_{s,\frac{\ell-1}{2}}^{\diamond}
and
F_{s-1,\frac{\ell-1}{2}}^{\diamond}
with
s>\tau(K)
, and the result on the row consisting of
E_{s,\frac{\ell+1}{2}}^{\diamond}
and
F_{s,\frac{\ell+1}{2}}^{\diamond}
for
s<-\tau(K)
follows by a similar argument. As before, we consider length-
0
,
1
, and
2
differentials separately.

For the length-
0
differentials, consider possible differentials leaving
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}_{0}=\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}|\bm{\mathbf{e}}^{\free}_{\frac{1}{2}+b_{1},\frac{\ell-1}{2}}
in
E^{\diamond}_{\tau(K)+\frac{1}{2},\frac{\ell-1}{2}}
first. By the description of
\mathscr{E}^{\diamond}_{*,t}
in Figure
5.1
, the only case in which such a length-
0
differential could exist is when
b_{1}=-1
and
b_{2}<0
in the standard complex summand
C(b_{1},\dots,b_{m})
of
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}_{\hat{R}}(K)
. However, for knot
K
in
S^{3}
, such a standard complex is not realizable, see the last paragraph of
[
8
]
*Section 11. Other length-
0
differentials vanish due to the constraint on the
s
-coordinate and the structure of
\mathscr{E}^{\diamond}_{*,t}
and
\mathscr{F}^{\diamond}_{*,t}
depicted in Figure
5.1
.

For the length-
1
differentials, consider the vertical length-
1
differentials
\Phi^{\pm K}
first. Since
m\geq 2
in the standard complex summand
C(b_{1},\dots,b_{m})
of
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}_{\hat{R}}(K)
when
\varepsilon(K)\neq 0,
there are no
\sigma
-or
\tau
-weighted terms in
\delta^{1}_{1}(\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1})
in
\mathcal{X}_{n}(b_{1},\dots,b_{m})^{\hat{\mathcal{K}}}
. See Theorem
3.5
. Then, by the description of
f^{\pm K}
in Figure
5.1
, we have

\Phi^{\pm K}(\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}_{0})=\Phi^{\pm K}(\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0})=0.

The vanishing of the remaining vertical length-
1
differentials (i.e.,
\Phi^{\pm K}
) follows from the same proof of the corresponding statement in Lemma
5.5
.

Next, we consider horizontal length-
1
differentials
\Phi^{\pm\mu}
. By Figure
5.2
,
\Phi^{\mu}
acts by
\mathbb{I}\boxtimes L_{\sigma}
, and
\Phi^{-\mu}
acts by
\mathbb{I}\boxtimes L_{\tau}
.
We proceed in the same way as in the proof of corresponding part in Lemma
5.5
, except that we also need to show the map
\mathbb{I}\boxtimes L_{\sigma}
vanishes on
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}_{0}\in E_{\tau(K)+\frac{1}{2},\frac{\ell-1}{2}}^{\diamond}
, which is

L_{\sigma}\left(\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}_{0}\right)=0\in\mathcal{S}^{\diamond}.

By the assumptions

\ell\geq 0,\text{ and }R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}\geq g_{3}(P)+\frac{\ell}{2}-1,

it follows from Lemma
5.10
that the actions of
{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}^{\diamond}(L_{P})_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
satisfy

L_{\sigma}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\ell/2-1})=0

as desired.

For the row consisting of
E_{s,\frac{\ell+1}{2}}^{\diamond}
and
F_{s-1,\frac{\ell+1}{2}}^{\diamond}
with
s<-\tau(K)
, we need
\mathbb{I}\boxtimes L_{\tau}
to vanish on
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{m-1}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}_{0}\in E_{-\tau(K)-\frac{1}{2},\frac{\ell+1}{2}}^{\diamond}
, which is

L_{\tau}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1})=0\in\mathcal{S}^{\diamond}.

Again, this follows from the assumptions and Lemma
5.10
.

There are no length 2 differentials by Lemma
5.1
, so the proof is complete.
∎

It is straightforward to see that the homology of
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime\prime}(P,K,n)
contains an infinite
Z
-tower. By the same technique as in Theorem
5.6
, we compute
\tau(P(K,n))
starting from the truncated
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime\prime}(P,K,n)
, and summarize the result in the following theorem.

Theorem 5.16
.

Suppose
L_{P}
is a
2
-component
L
-space link,
K
is a companion knot with
\varepsilon(K)=-1
. Let
\ell\geq 0
be the non-negative linking number
\ell=\mathrm{lk}(\mu,P)
of the two components of
L_{P}
. Suppose the following condition holds for the
H
-function of
L_{P}
:

(5.5)

R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}\geq g+\frac{\ell}{2}-1

Then, we have the following formula for
\tau(P(K,n))
:

\tau(P(K,n))=\begin{cases}\max\{R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}+\frac{\ell}{2},R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}-\frac{\ell}{2}\}+\frac{(\ell-1)\ell}{2}n+\ell\tau(K),&\text{if $n<2\tau(K),$}\\
\max\{R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}+\frac{\ell}{2},R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}-\frac{\ell}{2}\}+\frac{(\ell-1)\ell}{2}n+\ell\tau(K),&\text{if $n=2\tau(K)$. }\\
\min\{R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}+\frac{\ell}{2},R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}+\frac{\ell}{2}\}+\frac{(\ell-1)\ell}{2}n+\ell\tau(K),&\text{if $n=2\tau(K)+1$. }\\
\min\{R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}+\frac{\ell}{2},g+\ell\}+\frac{(\ell-1)\ell}{2}n+\ell\tau(K),&\text{if $n>2\tau(K)+1$. }\end{cases}

In the above,
g=g_{3}(P)
is the genus of the pattern
P
viewed as a knot in
S^{3}
.

Proof.

As in the case when
\varepsilon(K)=1
, the truncation of
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime\prime}(P,K,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
takes slightly different form depending on the relative values of
n
and
2\tau(K)
, as illustrated in Figures
5.6
and
5.7
.

The answer divides into the following four cases:

(1)

When
n<2\tau(K)
, the truncated
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime\prime}(P,K,n)
is homotopy equivalent to the following complex (canceling two pairs of identity maps in Figure
5.6
),

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{m-1}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{\tau}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2\tau(K)-n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{\tau}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{\tau}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{\tau}}

where

L_{\sigma}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{t}_{0})=\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0}\cdot Z^{R_{t}-\frac{\ell}{2}-g}\quad\text{ for }t\in\left\{\tfrac{\ell}{2},\tfrac{\ell}{2}+1\right\},

and

L_{\tau}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{t}_{0})=\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0}\cdot Z^{R_{t}+\frac{\ell}{2}-g}\quad\text{ for }t\in\left\{\tfrac{\ell}{2}-1,\tfrac{\ell}{2}\right\}.

There are
2\tau(K)-n
copies of
\bm{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}
in the top layer of the zigzag. The homology contains an infinite
Z
-tower, with the generator given by an appropriate linear combination of the top layer generators. Assuming
\ell\geq 0
, the generator of the tower has the same Alexander grading as
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}_{0}
if
R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}+\frac{\ell}{2}\geq R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}-\frac{\ell}{2},
or
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}_{0}\cdot Z^{R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}-R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}-\ell}
otherwise. This generator
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}_{0}=\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}|\bm{\mathbf{e}}^{\free}_{\frac{1}{2}+b_{1},\frac{\ell-1}{2}}
lies in
E^{\diamond}_{\tau(K)+\frac{1}{2},\frac{\ell-1}{2}}
. By the grading shift formula Equation
(9.5)
in
[
5
]
*Section 9.5, we have

(5.6)

A\left(\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}|\bm{\mathbf{e}}^{\free}_{\frac{1}{2}+b_{1},\frac{\ell-1}{2}}\right)=R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}+\frac{\ell}{2}+\frac{(\ell-1)\ell}{2}n+\ell\tau(K).

Therefore, in this case,

\tau(P(K,n))=\max\{R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}+\tfrac{\ell}{2},R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}-\tfrac{\ell}{2}\}+\frac{(\ell-1)\ell}{2}n+\ell\tau(K)

(2)

If
n=2\tau(K)
, then the truncated
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime\prime}(P(K,n))
is homotopy equivalent to

\hbox to126.49pt{\vbox to53.3pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 63.24374pt\lower-26.64839pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

with
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}_{0}=\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}|\bm{\mathbf{e}}^{\free}_{\frac{1}{2}+b_{1},\frac{\ell-1}{2}}\in E^{\diamond}_{\tau(K)+\frac{1}{2},\frac{\ell-1}{2}}
. In a similar manner to the previous case, we compute

\tau(P(K,n))=\max\left\{R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}+\tfrac{\ell}{2},R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}-\tfrac{\ell}{2}\right\}+\frac{(\ell-1)\ell}{2}n+\ell\tau(K).

(3)

If
n=2\tau(K)+1
, then after truncating
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime\prime}(P(K,n))^{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
as in Figure
5.7
, we see that it is homotopy equivalent to

\hbox to126.49pt{\vbox to53.28pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 63.24374pt\lower-26.63948pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-63.24374pt}{-20.47975pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{
{}{}{}}{}{

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

where

L_{W}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}_{0})=\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}_{0}\cdot Z^{R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}-R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}},\quad L_{Z}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}_{0})=\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}_{0}\cdot Z^{R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}-R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}}.

The bottom left element
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}_{0}
lies in
E_{\tau(K)+\frac{1}{2},\frac{\ell-1}{2}}^{\diamond}
, whose Alexander grading we have computed before in Equation (
5.6
), and the bottom right element
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{m-1}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}=\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{m-1}|\bm{\mathbf{e}}^{\free}_{-\frac{1}{2}+b_{m},\frac{\ell+1}{2}}
lies in
E_{-\tau(K)-\frac{1}{2},\frac{\ell+1}{2}}^{\diamond}
, and has Alexander grading

A(\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{m-1}|\bm{\mathbf{e}}^{\free}_{-\frac{1}{2}+b_{m},\frac{\ell+1}{2}})=R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}+\frac{\ell^{2}}{2}+\tau(K)\ell^{2}=R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}+\frac{\ell}{2}+\frac{(\ell-1)\ell}{2}n+\ell\tau(K).

Therefore, in this case,

\tau(P(K,n))=\min\left\{R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}+\tfrac{\ell}{2},R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}+\tfrac{\ell}{2}\right\}+\frac{(\ell-1)\ell}{2}n+\ell\tau(K).

(4)

If
n>2\tau(K)+1
, then the truncated
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime\prime}(P,K,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
in Figure
5.7
is homotopy equivalent to

}}{
}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{70.31111pt}{3.3617pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

with maps described as before. In particular,

L_{Z}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}_{0})=\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}_{0}\cdot Z^{R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}-R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}},\quad L_{\tau}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}_{0})=\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0}\cdot Z^{R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}+\frac{\ell}{2}-g},

and by Lemma
4.9
,

R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}\geq g-\frac{\ell}{2}.

There are
n-2\tau(K)
copies of
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{0}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}_{0}
in the top layer of the zigzag. Recall we have also assumed
\ell\geq 0.
Then, it is easy to show that the Alexander grading of
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{m-1}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}_{0}
is greater than or equal to each of the
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{0}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0}
generators in the bottom layer.

Therefore, the generator of an infinite
Z
-tower in the homology is either
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}
in
E^{\diamond}_{\tau(K)+\frac{1}{2},\frac{\ell-1}{2}}
, or the leftmost copy of
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{0}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0}
, which is
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{0}|\bm{\mathbf{f}}^{\free}_{\frac{1}{2},\frac{\ell-1}{2}}
in
F^{\diamond}_{\tau(K)+\frac{1}{2},\frac{\ell-1}{2}}.

The Alexander grading of
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}
has been computed before in Equation (
5.6
), and the Alexander grading of this copy of
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{0}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0}
is

A(\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{0}|\bm{\mathbf{f}}^{\free}_{\frac{1}{2},\frac{\ell-1}{2}})=g+\ell+\frac{(\ell-1)\ell}{2}n+\ell\tau(K).

Therefore, in this case,

\tau(P(K,n))=\min\left\{R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}+\tfrac{\ell}{2},g+\ell\right\}+\frac{(\ell-1)\ell}{2}n+\ell\tau(K).

∎

Remark 5.17
.

Here are two special families of L-space satellite operator that the extra assumption in Equation (
5.5
) is satisfied:

(1)

If the linking number
|\ell|\leq 1,
then by Lemma
4.9
, we have

R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}\geq g_{3}(P)-\frac{\ell}{2}\geq g_{3}(P)+\frac{\ell}{2}-1.

(2)

If the pattern knot
P
is unknotted when viewed as a knot in
S^{3}
, then
g=g_{3}(P)=0
. By Equation (
4.2
), we have

H_{L_{P}}(N_{L_{P}},r)=H_{L_{P}}(\infty,r)=H_{U}\left(r-\tfrac{\ell}{2}\right)=\begin{cases}\frac{\ell}{2}-r&\text{if $r<\frac{\ell}{2},$}\\
0,&\text{if $r\geq\frac{\ell}{2}$,}\end{cases}

while

H_{L_{P}}\left(\tfrac{\ell}{2}-1,R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}\right)=H_{L_{P}}\left(\tfrac{\ell}{2}-1,\infty\right)=1.

Since

\frac{\ell}{2}-1<\frac{\ell}{2}\leq N_{L_{P}},

by the monotonicity of the
H
-function in the horizontal direction, we have

R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}\geq\frac{\ell}{2}-1=g+\frac{\ell}{2}-1.

6.
The
\varepsilon
-invariant and non-surjectivity

In this section, we give an inequality for the
\varepsilon
-invariant of satellite knots under L-space satellite operations. More explicitly, we have the following.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
P
be an L-space satellite operator, and
g_{3}(P)
denote the Seifert genus of the pattern knot
P
. Orient the corresponding two-component link
L_{P}
such that the linking number
\ell\geq 0
. Then, we have the following inequality of
\varepsilon(P(K,n))
:

(1)

If
\varepsilon(K)=1
, or
\varepsilon(K)=0
and
n\geq 0
, then

\varepsilon(P(K,n))\neq-1.

(2)

Suppose further that the H-function of the two-component link
L_{P}
satisfies

(6.1)

R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}\geq g_{3}(P)+\frac{\ell}{2}.

Then, if
\varepsilon(K)=0
and
n<0
, or
\varepsilon(K)=-1
, we have

\varepsilon(P(K,n))\neq-1.

In particular, if the L-space satellite operator
P
satisfies the condition in Equation (
6.1
), then for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the map on the smooth concordance group

\displaystyle P:\mathscr{C}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathscr{C}

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle\longmapsto P(K,n)

is not surjective.

Remark 6.2
.

The condition in Equation (
6.1
) is slightly stronger (by 1) than the condition required for our computation of
\tau(P(K,n))
in Theorem
1.2
. It is not satisfied for cabling operations, but it is satisfied by all two-bridge links
L(rq-1,q)
with
r\geq q\geq 3
, where
r
and
q
are odd integers. This family includes the case of Whitehead double and Mazur pattern. It is also satisfied when
L_{P}
has linking number 0. It seems complicated to obtain an explicit formula of
\varepsilon(P(K,n))
. However, recall that the
\tau
-invariant of a knot must be
0
if the
\varepsilon
-invariant is
0
, so if we have
\tau(P(K,n))\neq 0
, then we can conclude that
\varepsilon(P(K,n))=1
under the assumption of the above theorem.

To study the
\varepsilon
-invariant, we need to consider the version of knot Floer homology over the ring
\hat{R}
. Recall the discussion after Definition
2.1
, the
\varepsilon
-invariant of a knot
K
is in
\{0,1\}
if there is a
\hat{R}
-local map from
\hat{R}
to
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}_{\hat{R}}(K)
, and it is in
\{-1,0\}
if there is a
\hat{R}
-local map in the opposite direction.

For our purposes, there is another description of the
\varepsilon
invariant which is more convenient. There is a natural map

(6.2)

Q\colon\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}_{\hat{R}}(K)\to\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}(K)

which sets
W
to
0
. Then
\varepsilon(K)\in\{0,1\}
if and only if the induced map on homology

Q_{*}\colon\mathcal{H\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}_{\hat{R}}(K)\to\mathcal{H\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}(K)

has a
\mathbb{F}[Z]
-tower generator of
\mathcal{H\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}(K)
in its image.

Additionally, one defines
\varepsilon(K)\in\{-1,0\}
if and only if
\varepsilon(-K)\in\{0,1\}
.

It is helpful to rephrase the above condition into the language of bimodules. We will view the knot Floer complex over
\hat{R}
as a type-
D
module over
\hat{R}
, denoted
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(K)^{\hat{R}}
. We view
\hat{R}
as a bimodule
{}_{\hat{R}}\hat{R}_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
, where
Z
acts on the right by inclusion of
\mathbb{F}[Z]
into
\hat{R}
. There is a bimodule morphism

q\colon{}_{\hat{R}}\hat{R}_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}\to{}_{\hat{R}}\mathbb{F}[Z]{}_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}

gotten by setting
W
to be equal to zero. The morphism
q
has
q_{i|1|j}=0
unless
i=j=0
. Then the map
Q
can be described as

(6.3)

Q=\mathbb{I}_{\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(K)}\boxtimes q.

To study the
\varepsilon
-invariant, we need to study the
DA
-bimodule
{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L_{P})^{\hat{R}}
. For computational purposes, it is helpful to study
{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L_{P})^{\hat{R}}\boxtimes{}_{\hat{R}}\hat{R}_{\hat{R}}
. Analogously to Lemma
4.10
, this bimodule admits a relatively simple minimal model:

Lemma 6.3
.

(1)

The bimodule
{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L_{P})^{\hat{R}}\boxtimes{}_{\hat{R}}\hat{R}_{\hat{R}}
is homotopy equivalent to an
AA
-bimodule
{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}^{\ddagger}(L_{P})_{\hat{R}}
which has only
m_{1|1|0}
and
m_{0|1|1}
non-trivial. The bimodule
{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L_{P})^{\hat{R}}\boxtimes{}_{\hat{R}}\hat{R}_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
is homotopy equivalent to the same module, with scalars restricted on the right.

(2)

There is an isomorphism of
(\mathcal{K},\mathbb{F}[Z])
-bimodules:

\mathcal{X}^{\diamond}(L_{P})\cong\mathcal{X}^{\ddagger}(L_{P})/(\mathcal{X}^{\ddagger}(L_{P})\cdot W).

(3)

With respect to the homotopy equivalences

\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(P(K,n))_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}\simeq\mathbb{X}^{\diamond}(P,K,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(P(K,n))_{\hat{R}}\simeq\mathbb{X}^{\ddagger}(P,K,n)_{\hat{R}},

the map

Q\colon\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}_{\hat{R}}(P(K,n))\to\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}(P(K,n))

from Equation (
6.3
) is chain homotopic to

\mathbb{I}_{\mathcal{X}(K)\boxtimes\mathcal{H}_{-}}\boxtimes Q_{L_{P}}

where
Q_{L_{P}}
is the quotient map

Q_{L_{P}}\colon\mathcal{X}^{\ddagger}(L_{P})\to\mathcal{X}^{\ddagger}(L_{P})/(\mathcal{X}^{\ddagger}(L_{P})\cdot W)\cong\mathcal{X}^{\diamond}(L_{P}).

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of Lemma
4.10
. Write
\mathcal{X}^{\ddagger}(L_{P})
for the right
\hat{R}
-module constructed by replacing each staircase
\mathcal{C}_{s}
or
\mathcal{S}
with
H_{*}(\mathcal{C}_{s}\otimes_{R}\hat{R})
.

Before proving the main claims, we describe some basic facts about staircase complexes. If
\mathcal{C}
is a staircase, write
\mathcal{C}^{\ddagger}
for
H_{*}(\mathcal{C}\otimes_{R}\hat{R})
.

It is straightforward to verify that if
\mathcal{C}
is a staircase, then
\mathcal{C}^{\ddagger}
is in fact isomorphic to
H_{*}(\mathcal{C})/U
. This follows from a more general fact. Suppose
S
is a ring,
a\in S
and
C
is a free chain complex over
S
such that there is a quasi-isomorphism from
C
to its homology
H_{*}(C)
. Suppose further that the action of
a
is an injection on
C
and
H_{*}(C)
. Then we claim that
C/a
is quasi-isomorphic to
H_{*}(C)/a
. This follows from the following sequence of quasi-isomorphisms:

(6.4)

\begin{split}C/a\simeq&(C\xrightarrow{a}C)\\
=&C\otimes(R\xrightarrow{a}R)\\
\simeq&H_{*}(C)\otimes(R\xrightarrow{a}R)\\
=&(H_{*}(C)\xrightarrow{a}H_{*}(C))\\
\simeq&H_{*}(C)/a.\end{split}

The first quasi-isomorphism follows from the general fact that if
f\colon A\to B
is an injective chain map, then
\Cone(f)
is quasi-isomorphic to
B/f(A)
. The second quasi-isomorphism (second to third line) follows from the fact that there is a quasi-isomorphism
C\to H_{*}(C)
and the fact that
R\xrightarrow{a}R
is a free chain complex. The final quasi-isomorphism follows from the fact that
a
acts injectively on
H_{*}(C)
.

We now address the claims in the statement. The proof is essentially the same as the proof of Lemma
4.10
. For each staircase
\mathcal{C}_{t}
, we pick a strong deformation retraction of chain complexes over
\mathbb{F}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{t}\otimes_{R}\hat{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{t}^{\ddagger}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}

such that
H
increases the algebraic grading by 1. This can be achieved by writing
\mathcal{C}_{t}
as a mapping cone
\mathcal{C}_{t}=\Cone(d\colon\mathcal{C}_{t}^{1}\to\mathcal{C}_{t}^{0})
. Then
\Pi
is projection of
\mathcal{C}_{t}^{0}
onto
\mathcal{C}_{t}^{0}/\im d
. The short exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{C}^{1}_{t}\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{C}^{0}_{t}\xrightarrow{\Pi}\mathcal{C}^{\ddagger}\to 0

splits over
\mathbb{F}
. The map
I
is the corresponding map from
\mathcal{C}^{\ddagger}
to
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{t}
in this splitting, while
H
is the map from
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{t}
to
\mathcal{C}^{1}_{t}
in this splitting. The homological perturbation lemma equips
\mathcal{X}^{\ddagger}(L_{P})
with the structure of an
AA
-bimodule, and the same argument as in Lemma
4.10
implies that only
m_{0|1|1}
and
m_{1|1|0}
are non-trivial.

For the second claim, it suffices to show the analogous claim for a single staircase
\mathcal{C}
. In this case, we repeatedly apply Equation (
6.4
) to see that

\mathcal{C}^{\diamond}:=H_{*}(\mathcal{C}/W)\cong H_{*}(\mathcal{C})/W\cong\left(H_{*}(\mathcal{C})/U\right)/W\cong\mathcal{C}^{\ddagger}/W.

To prove the final claim, one verifies that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L_{P})^{\hat{R}}\boxtimes{}_{\hat{R}}\hat{R}_{\hat{R}}\boxtimes{}^{\hat{R}}[\iota]_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}^{\ddagger}(L_{P})_{\hat{R}}\boxtimes{}^{\hat{R}}[\iota]_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L_{P})^{\mathbb{F}[Z]}\boxtimes{}_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}\mathbb{F}[Z]_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}^{\diamond}(L_{P})_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{I}\boxtimes q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi\boxtimes\mathbb{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{L_{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi\boxtimes\mathbb{I}}

We leave the verification to the reader, as it is straightforward. In the above,
\iota\colon\mathbb{F}[Z]\to\hat{R}
is the canonical inclusion, and
[\iota]
is the induced bimodules. Here we write
\Pi
for both the homotopy equivalence from
{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L_{P})^{\hat{R}}\boxtimes{}_{\hat{R}}\hat{R}_{\hat{R}}
to
{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}^{\ddagger}(L_{P})_{\hat{R}}
, as well as the homotopy equivalence of the
\diamond
-labeled modules. These are homotopy equivalences of
AA
-bimodules, and their only non-trivial term is
\Pi_{0|1|0}
.
∎

In Figure
6.1
, we illustrate the modules
H_{*}(\mathcal{C})
,
\mathcal{C}^{\ddagger}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\diamond}
when
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(T_{3,4})
, shown below:

(6.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{4}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}

Figure 6.1.
The modules
H_{*}(\mathcal{C})
,
\mathcal{C}^{\ddagger}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\diamond}
, when
\mathcal{C}
is the staircase complex
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(T_{3,4})
, shown above in Equation (
6.5
). In these diagrams, each shaded square denotes a generator over
\mathbb{F}
. The action of
W
moves left, while the action of
Z
moves upwards.

Proof of Theorem 9.1.

We will use the description of
\varepsilon
in terms of the map

Q\colon\mathcal{C\!F\!L}_{\hat{R}}(K)\to\mathcal{C\!F\!L}_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}(K)

from Equation (
6.2
).

By Lemma
6.3
, it suffices to show that the map

\mathbb{I}_{\mathcal{X}(K)\boxtimes\mathcal{H}_{-}}\boxtimes Q_{L_{P}}\colon\mathcal{H\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}_{\hat{R}}(P(K,n))\to\mathcal{H\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}(P(K,n))

has in its image (on the level of homology groups) a generator of the
\mathbb{F}[Z]
-tower of
\mathcal{H\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}(P(K,n))
.

In our analysis of
\tau(P(K,n))
, we identified a generator of the
\mathbb{F}[Z]
-tower of
\mathbb{X}(P,K,n)
. We will call this
\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{\tau}
. The
\hat{R}
-module generators of
\mathbb{X}^{\ddagger}(P,K,n)_{\hat{R}}
are the same as the
\mathbb{F}[Z]
-module generators of
\mathbb{X}^{\diamond}(P,K,n)_{\mathbb{F}[Z]}
. Namely, these consist of the generators
\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{t}_{2i}
in idempotent 0 for
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and
t\in\mathbb{Z}+\ell/2
, as well as the generators
T^{j}\otimes\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{2i}
in idempotent 1. In particular, we may view
\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{\tau}
as an element also in
\mathbb{X}^{\ddagger}(P,K,n)
. To show that
\varepsilon(P(K,n))\geq 0
, it suffices to show that
\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{\tau}
is a cycle in
\mathbb{X}^{\ddagger}(P,K,n)
. As with
\mathbb{X}^{\diamond}(P,K,n)
in Equation (
5.1
), the complex
\mathbb{X}^{\ddagger}(P,K,n)
can be decomposed into a hypercube

\mathbb{X}^{\ddagger}(P,K,n)_{\hat{R}}=\hbox to138.44pt{\vbox to68.13pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 70.50201pt\lower-34.836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
