where
\mathbb{E}^{\ddagger}
,
\mathbb{F}^{\ddagger}
,
\mathbb{J}^{\ddagger}
and
\mathbb{M}^{\ddagger}
are defined via the obvious modification of
\mathbb{E}^{\diamond}
,
\mathbb{J}^{\diamond}
, and so forth. Similar to Lemma
5.1
, there is no length 2 map to the hypercube. The maps
\Phi^{\pm K}
are obtained by composing the
\sigma
and
\tau
outputs of
\delta^{1}
of
\mathcal{X}_{n}(K)^{\mathcal{K}}
into the
\delta_{2}^{1}
of
\mathcal{H}_{-}
. The maps
\Phi^{\pm\mu}
are obtained by composing the
\sigma
and
\tau
-weighted components of
\delta_{1}^{1}
of
\mathcal{H}_{-}
with the map
\delta_{2}^{1}
of
{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}^{\ddagger}(L_{P})_{\hat{R}}
. Similar to the proof of Lemma
4.10
, the structure map
m_{1|1|0}(a,-)
of
{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}^{\ddagger}(L_{P})_{\hat{R}}
is obtained by first including a module generator into the full module
{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L_{P})^{R}\boxtimes R
, applying
\delta_{2}^{1}(a,-)
of this module, and then projecting
\mathcal{X}(L_{P})\otimes R
to
\mathcal{X}^{\ddagger}(L_{P})
. The relevant terms of the differential are of
{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L_{P})^{R}
are therefore those which are weighted by either
Z^{i}
or
W^{i}
, for some
i\geq 0
.

We can interpret our proof that
\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{\tau}
was a cycle in Section
5
amounted to showing that there are no
Z^{i}
-weighted differentials leaving
\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{\tau}
(under the respective hypotheses of these theorems).

In this lemma, it suffices to show that there are no
W^{i}
weighted differentials leaving
\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{\tau}
which survive when projected to
\mathbb{X}^{\ddagger}(P,K,n)
.

We will in fact show that there are no
W
-weighted differentials from any generators in the subspaces
\mathfrak{C}^{\ddagger}
,
(\mathfrak{C}^{\prime})^{\ddagger}
,
(\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime})^{\ddagger}
or
(\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime\prime})^{\ddagger}
to other generators of
\mathbb{X}^{\ddagger}(P,K,n)
.

We showed in Section
5
shows that there are no differentials weighted by
Z^{i}
from
\mathfrak{C}
to other generators of
\mathbb{X}^{\diamond}(P,K,n)
.
Our work shows the same result for
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}
when
\varepsilon(K)=0
and
n\geq 0
, and
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime}
when
n<0
as long as

R_{\ell/2-1}\geq g_{3}(P)+\frac{\ell}{2}-1

holds.
A similar statement holds for
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime\prime}
when
\varepsilon(K)=-1
and the above condition holds.

To disambiguate notation, we write
\mathfrak{C}^{\ddagger},

(\mathfrak{C}^{\prime})^{\ddagger}
(and so forth) for the
\hat{R}
-span of the generators that we used to define
\mathfrak{C}
,
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}
(and so forth).

Therefore, to conclude
\varepsilon(P(K,n))\neq-1
, it suffices to show there are no
W
-weighted differentials from
\mathfrak{C}^{\ddagger}
(respectively
(\mathfrak{C}^{\prime})^{\ddagger},(\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime})^{\ddagger},(\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime\prime})^{\ddagger}
) to other generators in
\mathbb{X}^{\ddagger}(P,K,n)
. This will imply that
\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{\tau}
is also a cycle in
\mathbb{X}^{\ddagger}(P,K,n)
so
\varepsilon(P(K,n))\geq 0
.

Suppose first that
\varepsilon(K)=1
, and consider the complex
\mathfrak{C}^{\ddagger}(P,K,n)
defined in Definition
5.2
.

We start with length-
0
maps first. For elements of the form
\bm{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}_{0}\in J^{\ddagger}_{s,\frac{\ell-1}{2}}
and
\bm{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0}\in M^{\ddagger}_{s,\frac{\ell-1}{2}}
, it is obvious, as each
J^{\ddagger}_{s,\frac{\ell-1}{2}}
(resp.
M^{\ddagger}_{s,\frac{\ell-1}{2}}
) is a copy of
\mathcal{C}^{\ddagger}_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}
(resp.
\mathcal{S}^{\ddagger}
), and there are no
W
-arrows leaving
\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}_{0}
(resp.
\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0}
) in
\mathcal{C}^{\ddagger}_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}
(resp.
\mathcal{S}^{\ddagger}
). For
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{0}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}_{0}\in E_{s,\frac{\ell-1}{2}}
for
s>\tau(K),
since
\delta^{1}(\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{0})=0
in the standard complex of the companion knot
K
, and there are no
W
-arrows leaving
\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}_{0}
in
\mathcal{C}^{\ddagger}_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}
, there are no
W
-arrows leaving
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{0}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}_{0}
as well. The situation is similar for
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{0}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0}\in F^{\ddagger}_{s,\frac{\ell-1}{2}}
for
s>-1+\tau(K),

\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{m}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}_{0}\in E^{\ddagger}_{s,\frac{\ell+1}{2}}
for
s<-\tau(K)
and
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{m}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0}\in F^{\ddagger}_{s,\frac{\ell+1}{2}}
for
s<-\tau(K).

For length-
1
maps, we start with horizontal ones. In the row of
J^{\ddagger}_{s,\frac{\ell-1}{2}},M^{\ddagger}_{s,\frac{\ell-1}{2}}
, we observe that

L_{\sigma}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}_{0})=\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0}\otimes Z^{R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}-g_{3}(P)-\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}},\quad L_{\tau}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}_{0})=\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0}\otimes Z^{R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}-g_{3}(P)+\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}},

by the computation in Lemma
4.17
. Therefore, there are no
W
-arrows leaving them. In the rows containing
E_{s,\frac{\ell\pm 1}{2}}^{\ddagger}
and
F^{\ddagger}_{s,\frac{\ell\pm 1}{2}}
, the argument is identical.

We now consider the maps
\Phi^{K}
and
\Phi^{-K}
(i.e. the vertical differentials). The maps
\Phi^{K}
or
\Phi^{-K}
vanish on
J_{s,\frac{\ell-1}{2}}^{\ddagger},M_{s,\frac{\ell-1}{2}}^{\ddagger}
, so we only need to consider the rows of
E_{s,\frac{\ell\pm 1}{2}}^{\ddagger}
and
F_{s,\frac{\ell\pm 1}{2}}^{\ddagger}
. For the row of
E_{s,\frac{\ell-1}{2}}^{\ddagger},F_{s,\frac{\ell-1}{2}}^{\ddagger},
we have already included all the downward maps
f^{K}
in the definition of
\mathfrak{C}(P,K,n)
. For the upward maps
\Phi^{-K},
since there is no
\tau
-term in
\delta^{1}(\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{0})
, there are no upward maps leaving
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{0}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}_{0}
or
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{0}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0}
. The situation for the row of
E_{s,\frac{\ell+1}{2}}^{\ddagger},F_{s,\frac{\ell+1}{2}}^{\ddagger}
is similar. This finishes the discussion when
\varepsilon(K)=1
.

When
\varepsilon(K)=0
and
n\geq 0
, consider the complex
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}(P,K,n)
in Definition
5.8
. There are no
W
-weighted arrows leaving
(\mathfrak{C}^{\prime})^{\ddagger}
, because it is easily verified that there are no internal differentials of any of the
\mathcal{C}_{t}^{\ddagger}
or
\mathcal{S}^{\ddagger}
leaving any of the generators of
(\mathfrak{C}^{\prime})^{\ddagger}
, and there are no possible
W
-weighted length differentials leaving
(\mathfrak{C}^{\prime})^{\ddagger}
since all
L_{\tau}
or
L_{\sigma}
arrows leaving a generator in this complex are already contained in the complex due to the shape of the complex, and they are all weighted by powers of
Z
. This finishes the discussion when
\varepsilon(K)=0
and
n>0
.

When
\varepsilon(K)=0
and
n<0
, consider the complex
(\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime})^{\ddagger}
, whose
\hat{R}
-generators are the same as the ones listed in Definition
5.8
. In this case, there are possibly non-trivial differentials from the copies of
\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}
and
\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}
. These are the left-most and right-most generators in Figure
5.5
. The possible differentials are

L_{\sigma}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1})\in\mathcal{S}^{\ddagger}\quad\text{and}\qquad L_{\tau}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1})\in\mathcal{S}^{\ddagger}.

Using computations similar to those in Lemma
5.10
and the assumption in Equation (
6.1
), we can choose a model of
DA
-bimodule
{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L_{P})^{\mathbb{F}[W,Z]}
, such that two maps to be

\delta_{2}^{1}(\tau,\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}_{0})=\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0}\otimes UZ^{R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}-g_{3}(P)+\frac{\ell}{2}},\quad\delta_{2}^{1}(\sigma,\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1})=\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}_{0}\otimes UZ^{R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}-g_{3}(P)-\frac{\ell}{2}}.

Observe that
\delta_{2}^{1}(\tau,\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1})
vanishes once we set
U=0
by our assumption in Equation (
6.1
). Also, we can see that
\delta_{2}^{1}(\sigma,\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1})
vanishes once we set
U=0
by observing that

R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}\geq g_{3}(P)+\frac{\ell}{2}\geq g_{3}(P)-\frac{\ell}{2}

by Lemma
4.9
and the assumption
\ell\geq 0
. So these maps vanish as well when working over the ring
\mathbb{F}[W,Z]/(U)
. This finishes the case
\varepsilon(K)=0,n<0
.

When
\varepsilon(K)=-1
, consider the complex
(\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime\prime})^{\ddagger}
which is generated over
\hat{R}
by the generators appearing in Definition
5.14
. Our argument is similar to the argument when
\varepsilon(K)=1
, in the previous paragraphs, though we must pay extra attention to the four extra generators.

To see that there are no length 0 differentials leaving the generator
\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{\tau}
, we argue similarly to our proof in Lemma
5.15
that
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime\prime}
is a subcomplex. There are no changes in the row containing
J_{s,\frac{\ell-1}{2}}^{\ddagger}
and
M_{s,\frac{\ell-1}{2}}^{\ddagger}
between
\mathfrak{C}^{\ddagger}
and
(\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime\prime})^{\ddagger}
. For the complexes
E_{s,\frac{\ell-1}{2}}^{\ddagger}
and
F_{s,\frac{\ell-1}{2}}^{\ddagger}
, the argument in Lemma
5.15
extends without significant change, as we now describe. We note that the complexes
\mathscr{E}_{*,t}^{\ddagger}
,
\mathscr{F}_{*,t}^{\ddagger}
,
\mathscr{J}_{*,t}^{\ddagger}
,
\mathscr{M}_{*,t}^{\ddagger}
and maps
f^{\pm\mu}
and
f^{\pm K}
take the same form as the corresponding
\diamond
labeled maps in Figures
5.1
and
5.2
. That is, we add no new arrows to these diagrams, and only replace the complexes and elementary maps (
L_{W}
,
L_{Z}
,
L_{\sigma}
and
L_{\tau}
) with the
\ddagger
versions instead of
\diamond
version. The situation is the same as in the proof of Lemma
5.15
: the only case in which a length-
0
differential could leave
(\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime\prime})^{\ddagger}
from generators in
E_{s,\frac{\ell-1}{2}}^{\ddagger}
and
F_{s,\frac{\ell-1}{2}}^{\ddagger}
would be if

b_{1}=-1
and
b_{2}<0
in the standard complex summand
C(b_{1},\dots,b_{m})
of
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}_{\hat{R}}(K)
. However, for knot
K
in
S^{3}
, such a standard complex is not realizable, see the last paragraph of
[
8
]
*Section 11. The situation for the row of
E_{s,\frac{\ell+1}{2}}^{\ddagger}
and
F_{s,\frac{\ell+1}{2}}^{\ddagger}
is the same.

For length-
1
arrows, we only need to consider those starting from the four extra generators
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\prime}
,
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}
,
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{m-1}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}
and
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{m-1}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}
. Since there are no
\sigma
or
\tau
-terms in
\delta^{1}_{1}(\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1})
or
\delta^{1}_{1}(\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{m-1})
in
\mathcal{X}_{n}(b_{1},\dots,b_{m})^{\hat{\mathcal{K}}}
, we do not need to consider the differentials
\Phi^{K}
or
\Phi^{-K}
. For the differentials
\Phi^{\mu}
and
\Phi^{-\mu}
, the possible arrows leaving
(\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime\prime})^{\ddagger}
are the leftward one from
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{m-1}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}_{0}\in E^{\ddagger}_{-\tau(K)-\frac{1}{2},\frac{\ell+1}{2}}
,
represented by

L_{\tau}\left(\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}_{0}\right)\in\mathcal{S}^{\ddagger},

and rightward one from
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1}|\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}_{0}\in E_{\tau(K)+\frac{1}{2},\frac{\ell-1}{2}}^{\ddagger}
, represented by

L_{\sigma}\left(\bm{\mathbf{x}}^{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}_{0}\right)\in\mathcal{S}^{\ddagger}.

Under the assumption that
\ell\geq 0
and that Equation (
6.1
) holds, these two elements vanish by a small modification to the argument in Lemma
5.10
.
∎

7.
Thurston norm and slice genus bounds

In this section, we investigate the relationship between the Thurston norm of L-space links and the 4-genus of satellites by L-space patterns. We introduce some notations. We write
g_{4}(K)
for the 4-ball genus of a knot
K\subseteq S^{3}
. If
P
is a winding number
\ell
satellite, then we write
g_{3}^{\rel}(P)
for the minimum genus of a smoothly embedded surface in
S^{1}\times D^{2}
which has boundary equal to
P
and
|\ell|
parallel translates of
S^{1}\times\{pt\}
.

In this section, we prove the following, which is Theorem
1.5
in the Introduction:

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
K\subseteq S^{3}
be a knot such that
g_{4}(K)=\tau(K)>0
and let
P
be an L-space satellite operator with winding number
\ell
. Then

g_{4}(P(K,0))=\tau(P(K,0))=|\ell|g_{4}(K)+g_{3}^{\rel}(P).

We note that the inequality

g_{4}(P(K,0))\leq|\ell|g_{4}(K)+g_{3}^{\rel}(P)

is easy, since we can construct a smoothly embedded surface in
B^{4}
which bounds
P(K,0)
by taking a genus
g_{3}^{\rel}(P)
surface in
S^{1}\times D^{2}
which bounds
P
and
|\ell|
copies of a 0-framed longitude of
S^{1}\times D^{2}
, and then capping each longitude in
B^{4}
with a parallel copy of a genus
g_{4}(K)
slice genus for
K
.

Since
\tau(P(K,0))\leq g_{4}(P(K,0))
by
[
33
]
, it suffices to show that

(7.1)

\tau(P(K,0))=|\ell|g_{4}(K)+g_{3}^{\rel}(P).

The key additional result that we need to prove is the following:

Proposition 7.2
.

Suppose that
P
is an L-space satellite operator with winding number
\ell
. Then

R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}=g_{3}^{\rel}(P)+|\ell|/2.

Our proof of Proposition
7.2
uses two key results. The first is Ozsváth and Szabó’s proof that link Floer homology detects the Thurston norm of the link complement
[
36
]
, and the second is Liu’s computation
[
28
]
of
\widehat{\mathit{HFL}}(L)
for a 2-component L-space link in terms of the
H
-function of
L
.

We additionally consider the slice genus of knots of the form
P(K,n)
when
P
is an L-space operator. We will write
g_{3}^{\rel}(P,n)
for the minimum genus of a smoothly embedded surface in
S^{1}\times D^{2}
with boundary equal to
P
and
|\ell|
parallel copies of a curve with homology class
-\lambda-n\mu
, where
\lambda
is a 0-framed longitude (oriented so that
P
is homologous to
\ell\lambda
) and
\mu
is an oriented meridian. Therefore
g_{3}^{\rel}(P,0)=g_{3}^{\rel}(P)
. To understand the slice genus of knots of the form
P(K,n)
, we will restrict to patterns which have width
N_{L_{P}}
equal to
|\ell|/2
.

We will prove the following:

Proposition 7.3
.

Suppose that
P
is an L-space satellite operator with winding number
\ell
.

(1)

The width
N_{L_{P}}
is equal to
|\ell|/2
if and only if
P
has wrapping number
|\ell|
(i.e.
P
intersects a meridianal disk of
S^{1}\times D^{2}
in exactly
|\ell|
points).

(2)

If
N_{L_{P}}=\ell/2\geq 0
and
n\geq 0
, then

g_{3}^{\rel}(P,-n)=g_{3}(P)+\frac{\ell(\ell-1)}{2}n.

One immediate corollary of the above result, together with our formula for
\tau(P(K,n))
in Theorem
1.2
, is the following:

Corollary 7.4
.

If
K
is a knot in
S^{3}
with
\tau(K)=g_{4}(K)>0
, and
n\geq 0
, then

g_{4}(P(K,n))=g_{3}^{\rel}(P,-n)+|\ell|g_{4}(K).

7.1.
Thurston norm and the
H
-function

Liu
[
28
]
proved that the
H
-function of a 2-component link detects the Thurston norm of the link complement. In this section, we use this result to prove several results which relate the
H
-function of L-space satellite operators to certain minimal genus problems.

Our most general result is the following:

Proposition 7.5
.

Suppose that
L=L_{1}\cup L_{2}
is a 2-component L-space link in
S^{3}
, with linking number
\ell
. Let

M=\min\{s_{1}:H_{L}(s,s_{2})=H_{L}(s+1,s_{2})\quad\forall s\geq s_{1}\textrm{ and }s_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}+\ell/2\}.

Then
M-|\ell|/2
is equal to the minimum genus of a embedded surface
F
in
S^{3}
which has
F\cap L_{1}=\partial F\cap L_{1}=L_{1}
, and such that
F\cap L_{2}
consists of
|\ell|
transverse intersection points.

To simplify the notation, we will write
g_{3}^{\rel}(L_{1};L_{2})
for the minimal genus in the statement.

There are two steps in our proof of the above. The first is to translate Ozsváth and Szabó’s result about the Thurston norm and link Floer homology
[
36
]
to obtain a statement about the minimal genus
g_{3}^{\rel}(L_{1};L_{2})
and the support of
\widehat{\mathit{HFL}}(L)
. As a second step, we translate this condition on
\widehat{\mathit{HFL}}(L)
to a statement about the
H
-function of
L
.

Lemma 7.6
.

If
L=L_{1}\cup L_{2}
is a two-component link in
S^{3}
, then

g_{3}^{\rel}(L_{1};L_{2})=\max_{(s_{1},s_{2})\in\mathbb{H}(L)|\widehat{\mathit{HFL}}(L,s_{1},s_{2})\neq 0}s_{1}-|\ell|/2.

Proof.

The result will follow from Ozsváth and Szabó’s result
[
36
]
that link Floer homology detects the Thurston norm, which we now recall.

If
L\subseteq S^{3}
is a link, and
h\in H_{2}(S^{3},L)
is a homology class, then
h
may always be represented by a smoothly embedded surface
F
in
S^{3}\setminus\nu(L)
with boundary on
\partial\nu(L)
, such that
[F]=h
. Given
h\in H_{2}(S^{3},L;\mathbb{Z})
, we define the
Thurston norm
of
h
to be

x(h):=\min_{F\hookrightarrow S^{3}\setminus\nu(L)|[F]=h}\chi_{-}(F)

where
\chi_{-}(F)
is obtained by writing
F
as a disjoint union of connected surfaces
F_{1}\cup\cdots\cup F_{n}
and setting

\chi_{-}(F):=\sum_{F_{i}|\chi(F_{i})\leq 0}-\chi(F_{i}).

Ozsváth and Szabó encode the Thurston norm as follows. We can naturally view
\mathbb{H}(L)
as being a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where
n=|L|
. Furthermore,
H_{1}(S^{3}\setminus L;\mathbb{R})
can be identified with
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, by identifying the
i
-th meridian
\mu_{i}
of
L
with the standard
i
-th unit vector in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Therefore, we view

\mathbb{H}(L)\subseteq H_{1}(S^{3}\setminus L;\mathbb{R}).

We set

y(h):=\max_{\bm{\mathbf{s}}\in\mathbb{H}(L)|\widehat{\mathit{HFL}}(L,\bm{\mathbf{s}})\neq 0}|\langle\bm{\mathbf{s}},h\rangle|.

Ozsváth and Szabó
[
36
]
prove that if
L
is an
n
-component link with no trivial components, then

(7.2)

x(h)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\langle h,\mu_{i}\rangle|=2y(h).

In the above, we identify
h\in H_{2}(S^{3},L)
with its Poincaré dual, which lies in
H^{1}(S^{3}\setminus\nu(L))
.

Note that if
L
contains a trivial component, then the claim is straightforward, so we assume that
L
does not contain a trivial component.

We now return to the setting of two-component links. We consider the homology class
h\in H_{2}(S^{3},L)
of surfaces which have boundary equal to
L_{1}
, and which intersect
L_{2}
algebraically in
\ell
-points. Equivalently, we can view these as embedded surfaces
F
in
S^{3}\setminus\nu(L)
which have boundary equal to a longitude of
L_{1}
and some number of meridians of
L_{2}
. If two meridians along
L_{2}
have different orientations, then we may tube them together without changing
\chi_{-}(F)
. Furthermore, any closed component may be deleting without increasing
\chi_{-}(F)
. Therefore, we may assume that
F
is a connected surface with boundary equal to a longitude of
L_{1}
and exactly
|\ell|
meridians of
L_{2}
. The quantity
\chi_{-}(F)
is minimized when
g(F)
is minimized. We compute

\chi_{-}(F)=-\chi(F)=2g(F)-1+|\ell|.

On the other hand, we have

\sum_{i=1}^{2}|\langle h,\mu_{i}\rangle|=1\quad\text{and}\quad 2y(h)=\max_{(s_{1},s_{2})\in\mathbb{H}(L)|\widehat{\mathit{HFL}}(L,s_{1},s_{2})\neq 0}2|s_{1}|.

Applying Equation (
7.2
), we obtain that

2g(F)-1+|\ell|+1=\max_{(s_{1},s_{2})\in\mathbb{H}(L)|\widehat{\mathit{HFL}}(L,s_{1},s_{2})\neq 0}2|s_{1}|.

Rearranging, and noting that

\max_{(s_{1},s_{2})\in\mathbb{H}(L)|\widehat{\mathit{HFL}}(L,s_{1},s_{2})\neq 0}s_{1}=\max_{(s_{1},s_{2})\in\mathbb{H}(L)|\widehat{\mathit{HFL}}(L,s_{1},s_{2})\neq 0}|s_{1}|

by the symmetry of link Floer homology, we obtain

g(F)=\max_{(s_{1},s_{2})\in\mathbb{H}(L)|\widehat{\mathit{HFL}}(L,s_{1},s_{2})\neq 0}s_{1}-|\ell|/2,

which was the claim.
∎

Next, we relate the support of
\widehat{\mathit{HFL}}(L)
to the
H
-function of
L
, when
L
is an L-space link.

Lemma 7.7
.

Suppose that
L
is a 2-component L-space link with linking number
\ell
. Let

N=\min\{s_{1}:H_{L}(s,s_{2})=H_{L}(s+1,s_{2})\quad\forall s\geq s_{1}\textrm{ and }s_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}+\ell/2\}.

(This coincides with our definition in Definition
4.5
of
N_{L_{P}}
when
L=L_{P}
).
Then

N=\max_{(s_{1},s_{2})\in\mathbb{H}(L)|\widehat{\mathit{HFL}}(L,s_{1},s_{2})\neq 0}s_{1}.

Proof.

To align our exposition with our previous results about L-space links, we describe our proof in terms of the formality of
\widehat{\mathit{HFL}}(L)
, though the result may also be derived from Liu’s argument in
[
28
]
using a spectral sequence which converges to
\widehat{\mathit{HFL}}(L)
, which is defined using the
H
-function of
L
.

Consider the set
S=\{s_{2}:H_{L}(N-1,s_{2})=H_{L}(N,s_{2})+1\}
. The set
S
is non-empty by definition of
N
. Let
h
be the minimum value of
H_{L}(N,s_{2})
for
s_{2}\in S
, and let
n_{2}
be the minimum value of
s_{2}\in S
so that
H_{L}(N,s_{2})=h
.

We claim that

(7.3)

H_{L}(N+1,n_{2})=h,\quad H_{L}(N,n_{2}+1)=h,\quad H_{L}(N,n_{2}-1)=h+1,\quad H_{L}(N-1,n_{2})=h+1.

To establish the above equation, write

H_{L}(N,n_{2}+1)=\mathscr{N},\quad H_{L}(N+1,n_{2})=\mathscr{E},\quad H_{L}(N,n_{2}-1)=\mathscr{S},\quad H_{L}(N-1,n_{2})=\mathscr{W}.

The fact that
\mathscr{E}=h
follows from the definition of
N
. Similarly the fact that
\mathscr{W}=h+1
follows from the definition of
n_{2}
. We observe that
\mathscr{N}\in\{h,h-1\}
by monotonicity and the bounded gap property of the
H
-function. If
\mathscr{N}=h-1
, then the monotonicity and bounded gap properties of the
H
-function imply that
H_{L}(N-1,n_{2}+1)=h
, which contradicts the definition of
h
. Hence
\mathscr{N}=h
. Similarly, we know that
\mathscr{S}\in\{h,h+1\}
. If
\mathscr{S}=h
, then
H_{L}(N-1,n_{2}-1)=h+1
by the aforementioned properties, which contradicts the definition of
n_{2}
. Therefore Equation (
7.3
) follows. A schematic is shown in Equation
7.4
.

(7.4)

\begin{array}[]{|c|cccccc|}\hline\cr\hbox{\diagbox[]{{\shortstack[l]{$s_{2}$}}}{{\shortstack[r]{$s_{1}$}}}}&\cdots&N-1&N&N+1&\cdots&\\
\hline\cr n_{2}+1&\cdots&*&h&*&\cdots&\\
n_{2}&\cdots&h+1&h&h&\cdots&\\
n_{2}-1&\cdots&*&h+1&*&\cdots&\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

We now use the above results to conclude

\begin{cases}\bigoplus_{s_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}+\ell/2}\widehat{\mathit{HFL}}(L,s_{1},s_{2})\neq 0&\text{ if }s_{1}=N,\\
\bigoplus_{s_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}+\ell/2}\widehat{\mathit{HFL}}(L,s_{1},s_{2})=0&\text{ if }s_{1}>N.\end{cases}

Since
L
is a 2-component L-space link, the complex
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}(L)
is formal by
[
5
]
*Theorem 1.2. Therefore,
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}(L)
, viewed as a type-
A
module over
R_{2}:=\mathbb{F}[W_{1},Z_{1},W_{2},Z_{2}]
, is homotopy equivalent to
{}_{R_{2}}\mathcal{H\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptL}(L)
, viewed as a type-
A
module with
m_{j}=0
if
j\neq 2
. In particular, since we can realize
\widehat{\mathit{CFL}}(L)
as the tensor product of
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}(L)
with
\mathbb{F}
(viewed as an
R_{2}
-module with trivial
W_{1}
,
Z_{1}
,
W_{2}
and
Z_{2}
actions), we can write
\widehat{\mathit{CFL}}(L)
as the tensor product over
R_{2}
of
\mathcal{H\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptL}(L)
with a free resolution of
\mathbb{F}
over
\mathbb{F}[W_{1},Z_{1},W_{2},Z_{2}]
. A free resolution of
\mathbb{F}
is given by the Koszul complex obtained by tensoring the four complexes of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}

where
a
is one of
W_{1},Z_{1},W_{2},Z_{2}
. Here, the element
a^{*}
is put in Alexander grading
A(a)
. We will write
\mathscr{K}_{W_{1},Z_{1},W_{2},Z_{2}}
for the tensor product of these Koszul complexes. We can view
\mathscr{K}_{W_{1},Z_{1},W_{2},Z_{2}}
as a type-
D
module over
R_{2}:=\mathbb{F}[W_{1},Z_{1},W_{2},Z_{2}]
. Therefore we can write

(7.5)

\widehat{\mathit{CFL}}(L)\simeq\mathscr{K}^{R_{2}}_{W_{1},Z_{1},W_{2},Z_{2}}\boxtimes{}_{R_{2}}\mathcal{H\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptL}(L).

Compare to
[
3
]
*Equation 6.6.

If
\bm{\mathbf{s}}\in\mathbb{H}(L)
, let
\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{\bm{\mathbf{s}}}
denote
\mathbb{F}[U]
-tower generator of
\mathcal{H\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptL}(L_{P},\bm{\mathbf{s}})
. Note that
1^{*}\otimes\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{(N,n_{2})}
is a cycle. We claim that it is not a boundary. This follows from the fact that
\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{(N,n_{2})}
is not in the image of any of
W_{1}
,
Z_{1}
,
W_{2}
,
Z_{2}
by the form of the
H
-function shown in Equation (
7.4
). In particular,
\bigoplus_{s_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}+\ell/2}\widehat{\mathit{HFL}}(L,N,s_{2})\neq 0
.

We now claim that
\widehat{\mathit{HFL}}(L,s_{1},s_{2})=0
for all
s_{2}
and
s_{1}>N
. We can rewrite the model of
\widehat{\mathit{CFL}}(L_{P})
from Equation (
7.5
) as

\Cone\left(\hbox to261.69pt{\vbox to23.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 130.84357pt\lower-8.84027pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-130.84357pt}{-2.31946pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.58 0.25 L 32.69 0.25}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{23.82225pt}{0.18054pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-5.14864pt}{5.03331pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right).

Here
\{0,1\}
denotes an upward shift in the multivariate Alexander grading by
(0,1)
.

We recall an elementary fact that if
A
and
B
are chain complexes and
f\colon A\to B
is an injective chain map, then
\Cone(A\xrightarrow{f}B)
is quasi-isomorphic to
B/f(A)
. Since
Z_{1}
acts injectively on
\mathcal{H\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptL}(L)
, the above complex is quasi-isomorphic to

(7.6)

\mathscr{K}_{W_{1},W_{2},Z_{2}}\boxtimes(\mathcal{H\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptL}(L)/Z_{1}\mathcal{H\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptL}(L)).

However, the image of the action of
Z_{1}
on
\mathcal{H\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptL}(L)
contains the entire subspace of elements which lie in
A_{1}
-Alexander grading above
N
. This is because the
H
-function satisfies
H_{L}(s_{1}+1,s_{2})=H_{L}(s_{1},s_{2})
for any
s_{1}\geq N
. Since
\mathscr{K}_{W_{1},W_{2},Z_{2}}
is supported only in non-positive
A_{1}
-Alexander gradings, it follows that Equation (
7.6
) has no elements in
A_{1}
-Alexander gradings above
N
, completing the proof.
∎

Note that Proposition
7.5
follows immediate from Lemmas
7.6
and
7.7
.

We now apply Proposition
7.5
to a link of the form
L_{P}
, for an L-space pattern
P
. There are two ways to apply it, depending on which component we label as
L_{1}
or
L_{2}
. We obtain the following:

Corollary 7.8
.

Suppose that
P
is an L-space satellite operator.

(1)

The quantity
N_{L_{P}}-|\ell|/2
is equal to the minimum genus of an embedded, oriented surface in
S^{3}
with boundary
\mu
and which intersects
P
in exactly
|\ell|
points, transversely.

(2)

The quantity
R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}-|\ell|/2
is equal to the minimum genus of an embedded, oriented surface in
S^{1}\times D^{2}
which has boundary equal to
P
and to
|\ell|
parallel copies of a 0-framed longitude on the boundary of
S^{1}\times D^{2}
. (Previously, we wrote
g_{3}^{\rel}(P)
for this quantity).

The second part of the above Corollary is stated as Proposition
1.3
in the introduction and Proposition
7.2
in this section.

7.2.
Slice genus bounds

We now prove the main theorem of this section:

Proof of Theorem
7.1
.

Let
K
be a knot with
\tau(K)=g_{4}(K)>0
, and
P
be an L-space satellite operator. We may assume, by possibly reversing the string orientation of
P
, that
\ell=w(P)\geq 0
.

As observed in Equation (
7.1
), it is sufficient to show that
\tau(P(K,0))
is equal to
g_{3}^{\rel}(P)+\ell g_{4}(K)
.

By
[
16
]
*Corollary 4, we know that
\varepsilon(K)=1
. Therefore our Theorem
1.2
implies that

\tau(P(K,0))=R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}-\ell/2+\ell\tau(K).

By Corollary
7.8
the above is equal to

g_{3}^{\rel}(P)+\ell\tau(K)=g_{3}^{\rel}(P)+\ell g_{4}(K),

completing the proof.
∎

We also have the following statement when
\ell=0
, which is stated as Proposition
1.6
in the introduction:

Proposition 7.9
.

Suppose that
K\subseteq S^{3}
is a knot with
g_{4}(K)=\tau(K)>0
, and
P
is an L-space satellite operator with winding number 0. Then

g_{4}(P(K,n))=g_{3}^{\rel}(P)

for all
n<2\tau(K)
.

Proof.

Since the winding number is zero,
g_{3}^{\rel}(P)
is the minimum genus of a surface in
S^{1}\times D^{2}
which has boundary equal to
P\subseteq S^{1}\times D^{2}
. Therefore
g_{4}(P(K,n))\leq g_{3}^{\rel}(P)
for any
n
. Furthermore, since
\tau(K)=g_{4}(K)>0
, we have that
\varepsilon(K)=1
, as before. When
n<2\tau(K)
, the Theorem
5.6
and Corollary
7.8
imply that

\tau(P(K,n))=g_{3}^{\rel}(P).

Therefore
g_{4}(P(K,n))=\tau(P(K,n))
, completing the proof.
∎

7.3.
Patterns
P
with minimal wrapping number

If
P
is a satellite pattern, the
wrapping number
of
P
is defined to be the minimal geometric intersection number of
P
with a meridianal disk of
S^{1}\times D^{2}
. In this section, we study L-space patterns
P
with wrapping number
|\ell|/2
. By Corollary
7.8
, these are exactly the patterns with width
N_{L_{P}}
equal to
|\ell|/2
. In particular, any pattern where
P
is braided about
\mu
satisfies this condition (in particular cables and 1-bridge braids satisfy this condition).

Recall that
g_{3}^{\rel}(P,n)
is the minimum genus of a smoothly embedded surface in
S^{1}\times D^{2}
with boundary equal to
P
and
|\ell|
parallel copies of a curve with homology class
-\lambda-n\mu
, where
\lambda
is a 0-framed longitude (oriented so that
P
is homologous to
\ell\lambda
) and
\mu
is an oriented meridian. In this section, we prove the following:

Lemma 7.10
.

Suppose that
P
is an L-space pattern with
\ell\geq 0
and with width
N=\ell/2
. Then, for any
n\geq 0
, we have

g_{3}^{\rel}(P,-n)=g_{3}(P)+\frac{\ell(\ell-1)}{2}n.

Proof.

We first claim that
g_{3}^{\rel}(P,-n)
is equal to the maximum value of
s_{2}+n\ell s_{1}
for
(s_{1},s_{2})
in the support of
\widehat{\mathit{HFL}}(L_{P})
.

The quantity
g_{3}^{\rel}(P,-n)
measures embedded surfaces
F
in
S^{1}\times D^{2}
with boundary equal to the union of
P
and
\ell
parallel curves which have homology class
-\ell\lambda+n\mu
, where
-\lambda
is a 0-framed longitude (which we can think of as a meridian of the component
\mu\subseteq L_{P}
). If
h
is the class of this surface, we observe, using similar reasoning as in Lemma
7.6
, that a smoothly embedded surface
F
representing
h
minimizes
\chi_{-}(h)
if and only if it minimizes
g(F)
. Therefore we have

x(h)=2g(F)+\ell-1.

We also compute that

\sum_{i=1}^{2}|\langle h,\mu_{i}\rangle|=1+n\ell\quad\text{and}\quad\langle\bm{\mathbf{s}},h\rangle=s_{1}n\ell+s_{2}.

Therefore
y(h)
is the maximum of
s_{1}n\ell+s_{2}
ranging over
(s_{1},s_{2})
which are supported by
\widehat{\mathit{HFL}}(L_{P})
.

Note that since
N=\ell/2
, we must have
R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}=g_{3}(P)+\ell/2
by the stabilization property of the
H
-function, stated in Lemma
2.6
.

It follows from Lemma
7.7
(applied once with
L_{1}=\mu
,
L_{2}=P
and once with these roles reversed) that if
L_{P}
has width
N=|\ell|/2
, there are no points
(s_{1},s_{2})
in the support of
\widehat{\mathit{HFL}}(L_{P})
with
s_{1}>\ell/2
or
s_{2}>g_{3}(P)+\ell/2
. Since
n,\ell\geq 0
, we conclude that if
(s_{1},s_{2})
is in the support of
\widehat{\mathit{HFL}}(L_{P})
, then

(7.7)

s_{1}n\ell+s_{2}\leq(\ell/2)n\ell+g_{3}(P)+\ell/2.

On the other hand,
(\ell/2,g_{3}(P)+\ell/2)
is in the support of
\widehat{\mathit{HFL}}(L_{P})
by the proof of Lemma
7.7
, (because the point
(N,n_{2})
in the proof of the aforementioned lemma is clearly equal in this case to
(\ell/2,g_{3}(P)+\ell/2)
), and
(\ell/2,g_{3}(P)+\ell/2)
achieves equality in Equation (
7.7
), so we conclude

y(h)=\ell^{2}n/2+g_{3}(P)+\ell/2.

Using Ozsváth and Szabó’s Thurston norm detection result, stated above in Equation (
7.2
), we have

2g(F)=\ell^{2}n+2g_{3}(P)+\ell-1-n\ell-\ell+1=\ell(\ell-1)n+g_{3}(P),

as claimed.
∎

Remark 7.11
.

The case that
n=0
is of particular importance. Combining Corollaries
7.8
and Lemma
7.10
, we obtain

R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}-\ell/2=g_{3}^{\rel}(P)=g_{3}(P).

This is stated as Proposition
1.7
in the introduction.

8.
Examples

In this section, we illustrate the formulas for
\tau(P(K,n))
using three families of examples: the cabling operation,
1
-bridge braid, and the generalized Mazur patterns.

8.1.
Cables

If
p,q\in\mathbb{Z}
are non-zero integers with
\gcd(p,q)=1
and
p>0
, then we write
K_{p,q}
for the
(p,q)
-cable of
K
. We take the convention that
K_{p,q}
winds
p
times longitudinally and
q
times meridianally in the boundary of a tubular neighborhood of
K
. We write
q=np+r
where
0<r<p
. We let
P
be the cabling operator where
L_{P}
is obtained by taking the
(p,r)
cable of one component of the Hopf link. Then

K_{p,q}=P(K,n).

Since the cabling operator is braided in
S^{1}\times D^{2}
, we know by Corollary
7.8
that

N=\ell/2=p/2,\quad R_{\ell/2}-\ell/2=g_{3}(P)=\frac{(p-1)(r-1)}{2}.

If
\varepsilon(K)>0
, Theorem
1.2
implies

\tau(P(K,n))=g_{3}(P)+\frac{(p-1)p}{2}n+p\tau(K)=\frac{(p-1)(q-1)}{2}+p\tau(K).

When
\varepsilon(K)<0
, Theorem
1.2
does not apply directly, since the condition
R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}\geq g_{3}(P)+\ell/2-1
is typically not satisfied. Instead, we can use duality by observing that

-K_{p,q}=(-K)_{p,-q}

and that
\varepsilon(-K)>0
. Using the result in this case, we obtain

\tau(K_{p,q})=-\tau(-K_{p,q})=-\tau((-K)_{p,-q})=\frac{(p-1)(q+1)}{2}+p\tau(K),

recovering Hom’s formula from
[
16
]
. Note that the case that
\varepsilon(K)=0
is straightforward because the knot Floer complex of
K
is locally equivalent to the knot Floer complex of the unknot, so we see that

\tau(K_{p,q})=\tau(U_{p,q})=\tau(T_{p,q})=\begin{cases}\frac{(p-1)(q-1)}{2}&\text{ if }q>0\\
\frac{(p-1)(q+1)}{2}&\text{ if }q<0.\end{cases}

in this case.

8.2.
1-bridge braids

Let
p,q,b
be integers such that
p\geq 2
,
q\neq np
or
-1+np
for some
n
, (otherwise we are guaranteed to get a link for the following braid closure), and
0<b<p-1
. (If
b=0
or
b=p-1
, then
B(p,q,b)
is a torus knot). Let
B(p,q,b)
be the braid closure of
(\sigma_{b}\sigma_{b-1}\cdots\sigma_{1})(\sigma_{p-1}\sigma_{p-2}\cdots\sigma_{1})^{q}
. Greene, Lewallen and Vafaee proved in
[
11
]
that when the braid closure is a knot, then
B(p,q,b)
is an L-space knot when
q\geq 1
. From now on, we only consider the case when the braid closure
B(p,q,b)
gives a knot. These knots are called
1-bridge braids
. This family (together with torus knots) includes the
Berge-Gabai knots

[
9
]
, which are the knots in the solid torus which admit a solid torus surgery.

Let
L(p,q,b)=B(p,q,b)\cup\mu
be the corresponding
2
-component link by viewing
B(p,q,b)
as lying in the solid torus, and
\mu
denotes the meridian of the solid torus. Note that adding a positive full twist along
\mu
changes
B(p,q,b)
to
B(p,q+p,b)
. Therefore, by
[
5
]
*Lemma 6.2 (see also
[
29
]
*Lemma 2.6),
L(p,q,b)
is an L-space link when
q\geq p+1
. See Figure
8.1
for an example.

Figure 8.1.
The link
L(4,5,2)
.

Let
P_{p,q,b}
be the satellite operation with the pattern
B_{p,q,b}
. Note that

P_{p,q,b}(K,n)=P_{p,q+pn,b}(K,0),

so it suffices to consider the case when
p<q<2p
, and other values of
q
could be deduced by adjusting the framing
n
appropriately. Write
q=r+pn
, with
p<r<2p
.

For the simplicity of notation, define

K_{p,q,b}:=P_{p,q,b}(K,0)=P_{p,r,b}(K,n).

In this example, we compute the formula for
\tau(K_{p,q,b})
using Theorem
1.2
, which recovers the result in
[
7
]
*Theorem 6.8.

By definition,
B(p,q,b)
is braided in
S^{1}\times D^{2}
, so by Corollary
7.8
, we have

N=\ell/2=p/2,\quad R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}-\ell/2=g_{3}(B(p,r,b))=\frac{(p-1)(r-1)+b}{2},

where the Seifert genus
g_{3}(B(p,r,b))
is easy to obtain as it is a positive knot.

If
\varepsilon(K)>0
, Theorem
1.2
implies

\tau(K_{p,q,b})=g_{3}(B(p,r,b))+\frac{(p-1)p}{2}n+p\tau(K)=\frac{(p-1)(q-1)+b}{2}+p\tau(K).

If
\varepsilon(K)<0
, again we can’t apply Theorem
1.2
directly for the same reason as in the cabling case. But we can use the same mirroring trick. Note that the mirror of
B(p,q,b)
is
B(p,-q-1,p-b-1),
so

-(K_{p,q,b})=(-K)_{p,-q-1,p-b-1},

where
-K
denote the mirror of a knot
K
. Therefore, when
\varepsilon(K)<0
, we have

\tau(K_{p,q,b})=-\tau(-(K_{p,q,b}))=-\tau((-K)_{p,-q-1,p-b-1})=\frac{(p-1)(q+1)+b}{2}+p\tau(K).

If
\varepsilon(K)=0
, we have

\tau(K_{p,q,b})=\tau(U_{p,q,b})=\tau(B(p,q,b))=\begin{cases}\frac{(p-1)(q-1)+b}{2}&\text{ if }q>0,\\
\frac{(p-1)(q+1)+b}{2}&\text{ if }q<-1,\end{cases}

using the fact that
B(p,q,b)
is an L-space knot when
q>0
, and the same mirror trick for the
q<-1
case. (When
q=0
or
-1
, the braid closure
B(p,q,b)
is a link instead of a knot.)

8.3.
Satellites by a family of
2
-bridge links

Consider
2
-bridge links of the form
L(rq-1,q)
, where
r
and
q
are positive odd integers (excluding the case
r=q=1
). Here we follow the convention in
[
20
]
for the slope of rational tangles, so that
L(rq-1,q)
corresponds to
K(\frac{rq-1}{q})
in their notation. By
[
41
]
, the (unoriented) link
L(rq-1,q)
is isotopic to
L(rq-1,r)
, so we may and will assume
r\geq q
by switching
r
and
q
if necessary in the rest of this example. See Figure
8.2
.

We denote by
P_{r,q}
the satellite operation such that the corresponding
2
-component link
L_{P_{r,q}}
is
L(rq-1,q)
. For example, the identity operator (for which the corresponding
2
-component link is the positive Hopf link) correspond to the case
r=3,q=1
, the (positive clapsed) Whitehead double corresponds to the case
r=q=3
, the Mazur pattern corresponds to
r=5,q=3
.

\frac{r-1}{2}
\frac{q-1}{2}

Figure 8.2.
The link
L(rq-1,q)
for odd integers
r\geq q>1
. The picture depicts the case when
r=9
and
q=7
.

In
[
29
]
*Theorem 3.8, Liu proved that each
L(rq-1,q)
, where
q
and
r
are positive odd integers, is a
2
-component L-space link; in his notation,
L(rq-1,q)
is denoted by
b(rq-1,-q)
. Since each component of
L(rq-1,q)
is an unknot, the extra condition in Equation (
1.2
) in Theorem
1.2
is satisfied by Remark
5.17
. In this example, we will compute the formula for
\tau(P_{q,r}(K,n))
using Theorem
1.2
. The main effort lies in writing down the multivariable Alexander polynomial of
L(rq-1,q)
, which we can fortunately use an algorithm developed by Hoste in
[
18
]
.

In
[
43
]
, Tuler gives a formula to the compute the linking number of
2
-bridge links. In particular, the linking number of
L(rq-1,q)
, with a specific orientation specified by the bridge presentation, is given by

\ell=\frac{r-q}{2},

which is non-negative since we have assumed
r\geq q
.

In
[
18
]
, Hoste gives an algorithm to compute the multivariable Alexander polynomials of
2
-bridge links
L(p,q)
with the same orientations specified by the bridge presentation as above. Note that
L(p,q)
corresponds to
K_{q/p}
in his convention. (The only parity of
p
and
q
such that
L(p,q)
is a two-component link instead of a knot is when
p
is even and
q
is odd, so there should be no danger of confusion with the convention used in
[
18
]
). The closed-form formula is

\Delta_{L(p,q)}(x_{1},x_{2})\doteq\sum_{i=1}^{p}\eta_{2i-1}x_{1}^{\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}\eta_{2j}}x_{2}^{\frac{\eta_{2i-1}-1}{2}+\sum_{k=1}^{i-1}\eta_{2k-1}},

where
\eta_{i}=(-1)^{\lfloor\frac{iq}{p}\rfloor}.

More interestingly, he gives a combinatorial description of the algorithm in terms of walks on a
2
-dimensional grid. See
[
18
]
*Algorithm 3. In the special case when
p=rq-1
, as considered here, the sequence of signs
\eta_{i}=(-1)^{\lfloor\frac{iq}{rq-1}\rfloor}
,
i=1,\dots,rq-2
, is given by
r-1
many
+
’s, followed by an alternating sequence of
r
many
-
’s and
r
many
+
’s, repeated
q-2
times, and ending with
r-1
many
+
’s. (When
q=1
, this sequence is just
r-1
many
+
’s.) For example, when
r=5,q=3
, this sequence
\left\{\eta_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{13}
is given by

++++-----++++.

(The linking number is obtained by summing up
\eta_{2k+1}
for
k=0,\dots,(rq-3)/2
.) The corresponding walk on the
2
-dimensional grid is as follows:

(1)

Start at (0,0), then move
(r-3)/2
steps of
(1,1)
(one unit right and one unit up).

(2)

Move one step of
(1,0)
(one unit right), followed by
(r-1)/2
steps of
(-1,-1)
(one unit left and one unit down), then one step of
(1,0)
, followed by
(r-3)/2
many steps of
(1,1)
.

(3)

Repeat step (2) for
(q-3)/2
more times. (If
q=1
, then stop after step (1).)

Then, if
k
is the number of times the walk visits the point
(i,j)
, the (unsymmetrized) Alexander polynomial will contain the term
(-1)^{i+j}kx_{1}^{i}x_{2}^{j}
, up to a global sign. In the case
p=rq-1
, each point is visited either
0
or
1
time, so every monomial in
\Delta_{L(rq-1,q)}(x_{1},x_{2})
has coefficient
\pm 1
, as it should be. Below is the example of the walk when
r=5,q=3
, which is the Mazur link.

The (unsymmetrized) Alexander polynomial of this example is then given by

\Delta_{L(14,3)}(x_{1},x_{2})\doteq 1+x_{1}x_{2}-x_{1}^{2}x_{2}-x_{1}\\
-x_{2}^{-1}+x_{1}x_{2}^{-1}+x_{1}^{2}.

In general, the support of the multivariable Alexander polynomial takes the following form in Figure
8.3
, assuming
r\geq q\geq 3
. When
r>3,q=1
, the walk simply consists of
(r-3)/2
steps of
(1,1)
, so the support of the multivariable Alexander polynomial is a single line segment of slope
1
. When
r=3
and
q=1
, the walk is the empty one, the Alexander polynomial consists of a single monomial
1
, and the link is the positive Hopf link.

Figure 8.3.
Support of the Alexander polynomial of
2
-bridge links
L(rq-1,q)

After symmetrizing and multiplying by
x_{1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}x_{2}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
, the monomial represented by the red dot in Figure
8.3
, which has the highest power of
x_{2}
in
\widetilde{\Delta}_{L(rq-1,q)}(x_{1},x_{2})
, corresponds to the monomial

-x_{1}^{(r-q+4)/4}x_{2}^{(r+q-2)/4}.

(The sign is chosen such that the
H
-function obtained below takes non-negative values.)

Recall the algorithm to obtain the
H
-function from
\widetilde{\Delta}
from Proposition
2.5
. We start with the stabilized state

J(t,r)=H_{L_{1}}(t-\ell/2)+H_{L_{2}}(t-\ell/2),

where in this case, both
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
are unknot. To form
H_{L}(t,r)
, for each non-trivial summand
a_{j,k}x_{1}^{j}x_{2}^{k}
in
\widetilde{\Delta}_{L}(x_{1},x_{2})
, we add
-a_{j,k}
to each of
J(t,r)
for
t<j,
and
r<k
. From the support of the nomalized alexander polynomial
\widetilde{\Delta}_{L(rq-1,q)}
, it is easy to see that

\begin{split}&H_{L(rq-1,q)}\left(\tfrac{\ell}{2}-1,\infty\right)=H_{L(rq-1,q)}\left(\tfrac{\ell}{2}-1,\tfrac{r+q-6}{4}\right)=1,\\
&H_{L(rq-1,q)}\left(\tfrac{\ell}{2}-1,\tfrac{r+q-10}{4}\right)=2,\\
&H_{L(rq-1,q)}\left(\tfrac{\ell}{2},\infty\right)=H_{L(rq-1,q)}\left(\tfrac{\ell}{2},\tfrac{r+q-2}{4}\right)=0,\\
&H_{L(rq-1,q)}\left(\tfrac{\ell}{2},\tfrac{r+q-6}{4}\right)=1,\\
&H_{L(rq-1,q)}\left(\tfrac{\ell}{2}+1,\infty\right)=H_{L(rq-1,q)}\left(\tfrac{\ell}{2}+1,\tfrac{r+q-6}{4}\right)=0,\\
&H_{L(rq-1,q)}\left(\tfrac{\ell}{2}+1,\tfrac{r+q-10}{4}\right)=1,\end{split}

where
\ell=(r-q)/2
. Therefore, we have

R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}=R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}=\frac{r+q-6}{4},\quad R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}=\frac{r+q-2}{4}.

By Theorem
1.2
, we have the following formula for
\tau(P_{r,q}(K,n))
(recall we have assumed
r\geq q
; otherwise, simply switch
r
and
q
):

(1)

If
\varepsilon(K)=1
, then

\tau(P_{r,q}(K,n))=\begin{cases}\frac{q-1}{2}+\frac{(\ell-1)\ell}{2}n+\ell\tau(K)&\text{if $n<2\tau(K)$,}\\
\frac{(\ell-1)\ell}{2}n+\ell\tau(K)&\text{if $n\geq 2\tau(K)$. }\end{cases}

(2)

If
\varepsilon(K)=0
, then

\tau(P_{r,q}(K,n))=\begin{cases}\max\{\frac{q-1}{2},\frac{r-3}{2}\}+\frac{(\ell-1)\ell}{2}n+\ell\tau(K)&\text{if $n<0$,}\\
\frac{(\ell-1)\ell}{2}n+\ell\tau(K)&\text{if $n\geq 0$.}\end{cases}

(3)

If
\varepsilon(K)=-1
, then

\tau(P_{r,q}(K,n))=\begin{cases}\max\{\frac{q-1}{2},\frac{r-3}{2}\}+\frac{(\ell-1)\ell}{2}n+\ell\tau(K)&\text{if $n<2\tau(K)$,}\\
\frac{r-3}{2}+\frac{(\ell-1)\ell}{2}n+\ell\tau(K)&\text{if $n=2\tau(K)$ or $2\tau(K)+1$,}\\
\min\{\frac{r-3}{2},\frac{r-q}{2}\}+\frac{(\ell-1)\ell}{2}n+\ell\tau(K)&\text{if $n>2\tau(K)+1$.}\\
\end{cases}

The family of two-bridge links we consider in this example is the same as the one studied in
[
38
]
, where
P_{r,q}(K,0)
corresponds to the satellite knot
Q_{\frac{r-1}{2},\frac{q-1}{2}}(K)
in their notation. See
[
38
]
*Proposition 4.3 for their parameterization of this family of two-bridge links. The difference is that we also take into account the effect of different framings of the companion knot, while they focus on the case when the framing
n=0
. In particular, when
r=q=3
, we recover the formula of
\tau
under Whitehead double in
[
14
]
. When
r=5,q=3
, we recover the formula of
\tau
for Mazur pattern in
[
22
]
. For
r=2j+3,q=3
, we recover the formula for
\tau
under the family
Q^{0,j}
in
[
1
]
.

9.
Homomorphism obstructions for satellite operators

In this section, we use our formulas for
\tau(P(K,n))
in Theorem
1.2
to partially confirm a conjecture by Hedden and Pinzón-Caicedo on when satellite operators act as a group homomorphism on the smooth concordance group for knots in
S^{3}
, under the extra assumption that
P
is an
L
-space satellite operation. We prove the following, which is stated as Theorem
1.9
in the introduction:

Theorem 9.1
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
denote the smooth concordance group of knots in
S^{3}
. If
L_{P}=\mu\cup P
is an
L
-space link, and the map

\displaystyle P:\mathscr{C}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathscr{C}

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle\longmapsto P(K,n)

is a group homomorphism for some
n\geq 0
, then

L_{P}
is the
2
-component unlink
U_{2}
, the positive Hopf link
\mathcal{H}_{+}
, or the negative Hopf link
\mathcal{H}_{-}
. Therefore,
P(-,n)
induces the trivial map, the identity map, or the orientation-reversing map on the concordance group.

Proof.

By
[
28
]
, we can compute
\widehat{\mathit{HFL}}
of a two-component L-space link from its
H
-function, and
\widehat{\mathit{HFL}}
detects the
2
-component unlink, the positive Hopf link
\mathcal{H}_{+}
, and the negative Hopf link
\mathcal{H}_{-}
. Therefore, it is enough to show the
H
-function
H_{L_{P}}
agrees with one of the
H
-functions
H_{U_{2}},H_{\mathcal{H}_{+}},H_{\mathcal{H}_{-}}.
See Figure
2.1
for the
H
-functions of the unlink
U_{2}
and positive/negative Hopf links
\mathcal{H}_{\pm}
respectively.

By reversing the orientation of
P
if necessary, we can assume the linking number
\ell=\mathrm{lk}(\mu,P)
to be non-negative, and we will prove
H_{L}=H_{U_{2}}
or
H_{\mathcal{H}_{+}}
under the assumptions in the theorem.

We will prove the case when
n=0
first. The proof for
n>0
follows from the same strategy, and we will indicate the necessary changes at the end of the proof.

Suppose the operator
P(-,0)
is a group homomorphism. We break the proof into a sequence of subclaims.

Claim 1
.

g_{3}(P)=0
and
R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}=\frac{\ell}{2}
. In particular, when viewed as a knot in
S^{3}
,
P
is an unknot.

The above claim is proven by applying Theorem
1.2
to knots of the form
\#^{j}K
which have
\varepsilon(K)=1
and
\tau(K)<0
as well as on knots which have
\varepsilon(K)=1
and
\tau(K)>0
. This implies that the constant term in the formula for
\tau(P(K,n))
in Theorem
5.6
must vanish, when applied to knots with
\varepsilon(K)=1
. The proves Claim
1
.

Claim 2
.

The width
N
satisfies
N=\ell/2
.

To prove the above claim, we consider
\frac{\ell}{2}\leq t
. From
R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}=\frac{\ell}{2}
(Claim
1
), together with the monotonicity result

R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}\geq R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}\geq\cdots\geq R_{N-1}\geq R_{N}=g_{3}(P)+\frac{\ell}{2}=\frac{\ell}{2}

from Lemma
4.9
, we conclude

R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}=R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}+1}=\cdots=R_{N-1}=R_{N}=\frac{\ell}{2}.

Here
N
denotes the width of
L_{P}
(Definition
4.5
).
Using the definition of
R_{t}
, this implies that

(9.1)

H_{L_{P}}(t,r)=H_{L_{P}}(t,\infty)=H_{\mu}(t-\tfrac{\ell}{2})=0\,\text{ for }\,t\geq\ell/2\,\text{ and }\,r\geq\ell/2.

Now, we consider
H_{L_{P}}(t,r)
for
\frac{\ell}{2}\leq t
, and
r<R_{t}=\frac{\ell}{2}.
By the bounded gap property of
H
-function in the
r
-coordinate (the vertical direction), we get

(9.2)

H_{L_{P}}(t,r)\leq H_{L_{P}}(t,\ell/2)+\frac{\ell}{2}-r=\frac{\ell}{2}-r,\text{ for }\frac{\ell}{2}\leq t\,\text{ and }\,r<\frac{\ell}{2}.

By the monotonicity property of
H
-function in the
t
-coordinate (the horizontal direction), we get

(9.3)

H_{L_{P}}(t,r)\geq H_{L_{P}}(N,r)=\frac{\ell}{2}-r,\text{ for }\frac{\ell}{2}\leq t,\,\text{ and }\,r<\frac{\ell}{2}.

Combining Equations (
9.2
) and (
9.3
), we conclude

(9.4)

H_{L_{P}}(t,r)=\frac{\ell}{2}-r,\,\text{ for }\,\frac{\ell}{2}\leq t,\,\text{ and }\,r<\frac{\ell}{2}.

From the definition of the width
N
(see Equation (
4.2
)), we see that Equations (
9.1
) and (
9.4
) imply that
N=\ell/2
, completing the proof of the claim.

Claim 3
.

R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}\geq\frac{\ell}{2}-1
.

By the definition of
R_{t}
, we have

(9.5)

H_{L_{P}}(\tfrac{\ell}{2}-1,R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1})=H_{L_{P}}(\tfrac{\ell}{2}-1,\infty)=H_{\mu}(-1)=1.

By monotonicity of the
H
-function in the
t
-direction, we have

1=H_{L_{P}}(\tfrac{\ell}{2}-1,R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1})\geq H_{L_{P}}(\tfrac{\ell}{2},R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1})=\frac{\ell}{2}-R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}.

Note that in the right-most equality in the above equation, we are using the fact that
N=\ell/2
, that
P
is an unknot, and the fact that
R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}\leq R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}
, which follows from Lemma
4.9
. Rearranging Equation (
9.5
) yields
R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}\geq\ell/2-1
, which was the claim.

Claim 4
.

R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}=R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-2}=\cdots=R_{-N}=-\tfrac{\ell}{2}
.

Claims
1
and
3
imply that

R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}\geq g_{3}(P)+\frac{\ell}{2}-1.

Therefore, the condition in Equation (
1.2
) in the
\varepsilon(K)<0
case of Theorem
1.2
is satisfied, so we can apply Theorem
1.2
to knots
K
with
\varepsilon(K)<0
. We apply Theorem
1.2
to knots of the form
\#^{j}K
where
\tau(K)>0
and
\varepsilon(K)<0
, and we conclude that

\max\{R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}+\tfrac{\ell}{2},R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}-\tfrac{\ell}{2}\}=\max\{R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}+\frac{\ell}{2},0\}=0.

Therefore

(9.6)

R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}\leq-\frac{\ell}{2}.

On the other hand, we apply Lemma
4.9
and use the fact that
g_{3}(P)=0
to see that

(9.7)

R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}\geq R_{\frac{\ell}{2}-2}\geq\cdots\geq R_{-N+1}\geq R_{-N}=g_{3}(P)-\frac{\ell}{2}=-\frac{\ell}{2}.

Combining Equations (
9.6
) and (
9.7
)
we conclude

R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}=R_{\frac{\ell}{2}-2}=\cdots=R_{-N+1}=R_{-N}=-\frac{\ell}{2},

as claimed.

Claim 5
.

The linking number
\ell
is either 0 or 1.

The above claim follows immediately from Claims
3
and
4
and the fact that
R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}-1}\leq R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}
by Lemma
4.9
.

Claim 6
.

If
\ell=0
, then
H_{P_{L}}=H_{U_{2}}
. If
\ell=1
, then
H_{P_{L}}=H_{\mathcal{H}_{+}}
.

In both cases, Claim
1
implies that the width
N
is
\ell/2
, which is either
0
or
1/2
. If
N=\ell=0
, then Equations (
9.1
) and (
9.4
) imply that
H_{L_{P}}(r,t)=H_{U_{2}}(r,t)
for
r\geq 0
. By symmetry of the
H
-function (see Lemma
2.6
) this implies that
H_{L_{P}}(r,t)=H_{U_{2}}(r,t)
for all
(r,t)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
. If
N=\ell/2=1/2
, the same argument works, since
H_{L_{P}}(r,t)=H_{\mathcal{H}_{+}}(r,t)
for all
r>0
by Equations (
9.1
) and (
9.4
), and symmetry implies equality for all
(r,t)\in(\mathbb{Z}+\tfrac{1}{2})\times(\mathbb{Z}+\tfrac{1}{2}).

This concludes the proof of the main theorem when
n=0
.

Claim 7
.

The main theorem holds also when
n>0
.

We suppose that
P(-,n)
is a homomorphism for some
n>0
. By considering companion knots of the form
\#^{j}K
with
\varepsilon(K)=1
and
\tau(K)>0
, as well as knots
K
with
\varepsilon(K)=1
and
\tau(K)<0
, we conclude that

R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}-\frac{\ell}{2}+\frac{(\ell-1)\ell}{2}n=0\quad\text{and}\quad g_{3}(P)+\frac{(\ell-1)\ell}{2}n=0.

Recall we have assumed that the linking number
\ell
was non-negative by choosing the orientation on
P
appropriately, and because

R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}-\frac{\ell}{2}\geq g_{3}(P)\geq 0,

the only possibility is

R_{\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{2}}}-\frac{\ell}{2}=g_{3}(P)=0,\text{ and }\ell=0\text{ or }1.

Then, the contribution of
n
in the formula for
\tau(P(K,n))
, which is
\frac{(\ell-1)\ell}{2}n
, becomes trivial, and we return to the same situation as when
n=0
.
∎

10.
A local inequality

In this section, we prove a result about the full knot Floer complex of L-space satellites of a knot
K
. We recall that if
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
are knots, a
local map
from
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(K_{1})
to
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(K_{2})
consists of a grading preserving chain map

F\colon\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(K_{1})\to\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(K_{2})

which becomes a homotopy equivalence after inverting
U=WZ
.

Theorem 10.1
.

Suppose that
P
an L-space satellite operation with linking number
\ell\geq 0
, and that
n
is an integer. Then for any knot
K\subseteq S^{3}
, there exists a
(\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}},\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})
-grading preserving local map

F\colon\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(P(K,n))\to\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}(K_{\ell,\ell n+1}).

As immediate corollaries, we obtain the following:

Corollary 10.2
.

The above implies inequalities for most concordance invariants. For example, we obtain that

(1)

\tau(P(K,n))\geq\tau(K_{\ell,\ell n+1})
.

(2)

V_{k}(P(K,n))\geq V_{k}(K_{\ell,\ell n+1})
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(3)

\Upsilon_{P(K,n)}(t)\leq\Upsilon_{K_{\ell,\ell n+1}}(t)
for all
t\in[0,2]
.

This is stated as Proposition
1.10
in the introduction. In the above,
V_{k}
denotes Rasmussen’s local
h
-invariant
[
40
]
, and
\Upsilon_{K}(t)
denotes the concordance invariant of Ozsváth, Stipsicz and Szabó
[
32
]
.

The following is stated as Corollary
1.11
in the introduction:

Corollary 10.3
.

If
P
is a winding number 0 L-space satellite operator, then

\tau(P(K,n))\geq 0,\quad\text{and}\quad\Upsilon_{P(K,n)}(t)\leq 0.

Proof.

When
\ell=0
, the knot
K_{\ell,\ell n+1}
is the unknot, so the result follows from Corollary
10.2
.
∎

We now prove Theorem
10.1
:

Proof of Theorem
10.1
.

The key step in our proof is to construct a morphism of
DA
-bimodules

f_{*}^{1}\colon{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L_{P})^{\mathbb{F}[W,Z]}\to{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(T(2,2\ell))^{\mathbb{F}[W,Z]}.

This map will be analogous to a local map between knot like complexes in that it becomes a homotopy equivalence after we invert
U
on the right (i.e. tensor with the rank 1
DA
bimodule corresponding to the inclusion of
\mathbb{F}[W,Z]
into
\mathbb{F}[W,Z,W^{-1},Z^{-1}]
). In particular, the map
F
will be

F=\mathbb{I}\boxtimes f_{*}^{1}\colon\mathcal{X}_{n}(K)^{\mathcal{K}}\boxtimes{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{H}_{-}^{\mathcal{K}}\boxtimes{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L_{P})^{\mathbb{F}[W,Z]}\to\mathcal{X}_{n}(K)^{\mathcal{K}}\boxtimes{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{H}_{-}^{\mathcal{K}}\boxtimes{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(T_{2,2\ell})^{\mathbb{F}[W,Z]}.

To construct
f_{*}^{1}
, we have to recall several important facts about staircase complexes. Let
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
be staircase complexes. Assume that on
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
, the
(\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}},\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})
-gradings take values in
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
, and that on
H_{*}(\mathcal{A})
and
H_{*}(\mathcal{B})
they take values in
2\mathbb{Z}\times 2\mathbb{Z}
(though we allow
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
to take non-standard gradings, in the sense of Definition
4.3
). Since
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are free-resolutions of their homology, there exists a grading preserving local map from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
if and only there is a grading preserving inclusion of
\mathbb{F}[W,Z]
-modules
H_{*}(\mathcal{A})\hookrightarrow H_{*}(\mathcal{B})
.

We can restate the condition that
H_{*}(\mathcal{A})\subseteq H_{*}(\mathcal{B})
in terms of an
H
-function of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
, as follows. We define
H_{\mathcal{A}}\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
by setting
H_{\mathcal{A}}(s)
to be the
-\frac{1}{2}
times the maximum grading of a
\mathbb{F}[U]
-non-torsion element in
H_{*}(\mathcal{A})
which lies in Alexander grading
s
. We define
H_{\mathcal{B}}
similarly. We observe that there is a grading preserving inclusion of
H_{*}(\mathcal{A})
into
H_{*}(\mathcal{B})
if and only if

(10.1)

H_{\mathcal{A}}(s)\geq H_{\mathcal{B}}(s)

for all
s\in\mathbb{Z}
.

We now define the component
f_{1}^{1}
of
f_{*}^{1}
. Write
\mathcal{C}_{s_{1}}
for the idempotent 0 complexes of
{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L_{P})^{\mathbb{F}[W,Z]}
, and write
\mathcal{C}_{s_{1}}^{\prime}
for the idempotent 0 complexes of
{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(T(2,2\ell))^{\mathbb{F}[W,Z]}
. By our above observations about staircase complexes, in order to construct
f_{1}^{1}
on left idempotent 0, it suffices to show that there is a grading preserving local map from each
\mathcal{C}_{s_{1}}
into
\mathcal{C}_{s_{1}}^{\prime}
. By the observation in Equation (
10.1
), it suffices to show that

(10.2)

H_{L_{p}}(s_{1},s_{2})\geq H_{T(2,2\ell)}(s_{1},s_{2})

for all
(s_{1},s_{2})\in\mathbb{H}(L_{P})=\mathbb{H}(T(2,2\ell)).
It is not hard to see that this also allows for the construction of the map
f_{1}^{1}
on left idempotent 1, because
\bm{\mathbf{I}}_{1}\cdot{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L_{P})^{\mathbb{F}[W,Z]}
can be identified with
\mathbb{F}[T,T^{-1}]\otimes\mathcal{C}_{s_{1}}
for
s_{1}\gg 0
, and similarly for
\bm{\mathbf{I}}_{1}\cdot{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(T(2,2\ell))^{\mathbb{F}[W,Z]}
.

To prove Equation (
10.2
), we use Lemma
2.6
and a direct computation of
H_{T(2,2\ell)}(s_{1},s_{2})
. We recall that

(10.3)

H_{T(2,2\ell)}(s_{1},s_{2})=\max\big\{H_{U}(s_{1}-\ell/2),-s_{1}-s_{2}+H_{U}(-s_{1}-\ell/2)\big\}.

See
[
5
]
*Section 6.1 for a description of
H_{T(2,2\ell)}
and the module
{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(T(2,2\ell))^{\mathbb{F}[W,Z]}
. Lemma
2.6
implies that

H_{L_{P}}(s_{1},s_{2})=H_{U}(s_{1}-\ell/2)

for
s_{2}\gg 0
. By the monotonicity property in Lemma
2.6
, we conclude that

H_{L_{P}}(s_{1},s_{2})\geq H_{U}(s_{1}-\ell/2)

for all
(s_{1},s_{2})
.
Using the symmetry property, we conclude that if
s_{2}\ll 0
, then

H_{L_{P}}(s_{1},s_{2})=-s_{1}-s_{2}+H_{L_{P}}(-s_{1},-s_{2})=-s_{1}-s_{2}+H_{U}(-s_{1}-\ell/2).

Using the bounded gap property, we conclude that

H_{L_{P}}(s_{1},s_{2})\geq-s_{1}-s_{2}+H_{U}(-s_{1}-\ell/2)

for all
(s_{1},s_{2})
. Using Equation (
10.3
), the inequality in Equation (
10.2
) follows.

We now describe how to construct the map
f_{j}^{1}
for
j>1
. The construction is essentially the same as the construction of the module
{}_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{X}(L_{P})^{\mathbb{F}[W,Z]}
from
[
5
]
*Section 5. If
a
is a monomial in the algebra, we want
f_{2}^{1}(a,-)
to satisfy

\delta_{1}^{1}\circ f_{2}^{1}(a,-)+f_{2}^{1}(a,\delta_{1}^{1}(-))=f_{1}^{1}\circ\delta_{2}^{1}(a,-)+\delta_{2}^{1}(a,f_{1}^{1}(-)).

The right hand side of the above equation is the sum of two chain maps from one staircase to another. These two chain maps have the same
(\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}},\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})
-grading, and are both local maps (i.e. become homotopy equivalences after we localize at
U
). By
[
5
]
*Lemma 5.6, the sum
f_{1}^{1}\circ\delta_{2}^{1}(a,-)+\delta_{2}^{1}(a,f_{1}^{1}(-))
is null-homotopic, so we pick
f_{2}^{1}(a,-)
to be any suitably
(\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}},\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})
-graded null-homotopy which increases the algebraic grading by 1. We now consider the
A_{\infty}
relations with two algebra inputs. Let
a
and
b
be monomials in
\mathcal{K}
. Then

\delta_{3}^{1}(a,b,f_{1}^{1}(-))+f_{1}^{1}(\delta_{3}^{1}(a,b,-))+f_{2}^{1}(a,\delta_{2}^{1}(b,-))+\delta_{2}^{1}(a,f_{2}^{1}(b,-))

is a chain map between two staircase complexes. Furthermore, this map increases algebraic grading by 1. By
[
5
]
*Lemma 5.8 this map is zero. Therefore the length 2 relations are satisfied. The
A_{\infty}
relations with
j>2
algebra inputs are similarly satisfied because the structure relation vanishes by algebraic grading considerations.

Finally, we note that the map
f_{*}^{1}
becomes a homotopy equivalence after inverting
U
, because the maps
f_{1}^{1}
do.
∎

Remark 10.4
.

More generally, it is straightforward to adapt the above proof to show the following. If
P
and
Q
are two L-space satellite operators with the same linking number
\ell
, and
H_{L_{P}}(t,r)\leq H_{L_{Q}}(t,r)
for all
t,r
, then

\tau(P(K,n))\geq\tau(Q(K,n))

for all knots
K
and integers
n
. Similar remarks hold for the
V_{k}
and
\Upsilon_{K}(t)
invariants.
